## The problem

The five seeded employees have their passwords written in plain text in `application/entitlement-assistant/resource/seed/fixtures.yaml` (for example `alex-demo-password`). The repository is public. If we seed Neon from that file and deploy to a public `workers.dev` URL, anyone who reads the repo can sign in as a CSR. The data is made up, but that still goes against your "security over speed" rule.

**Options:**
- **A (recommended):** seed production with passwords the seed command generates. They go into a git-ignored file next to `.env.neon`, and you share them privately with reviewers. This is a small change to the seed command: an optional git-ignored file of password overrides that production seeding must use. Local runs and tests keep the demo passwords, so the tests are unaffected.
- **B:** deploy with the published demo passwords. It's the fastest way and reviewers can sign in straight away, but anyone else can too.
- **C:** put Cloudflare Access in front of the URL. It's strong protection, but it needs more OAuth scopes and you'd have to manage who is allowed in.

## Deploy plan (once you've chosen)

I'll run each step so that no secret value appears in chat or in the published transcript. Values are read from git-ignored files and piped into the commands.

1. **Migrate Neon:** run `npm run db:migrate` with `DATABASE_URL` taken from `NEON_OWNER_URL` in `.env.neon`.
2. **Seed Neon:** run `npm run db:seed` the same way, with the generated passwords if you pick A.
3. **Login role:** create `entitlement_assistant_worker` as a member of `entitlement_assistant_app` (`IN ROLE`), with a generated password stored in `.env.neon`.
4. **Hyperdrive:** run `wrangler hyperdrive create entitlement-assistant … --caching-disabled` and put the returned id in `wrangler.jsonc`. If this fails on permissions, I'll tell you which one scope to add.
5. **Secrets:** generate fresh production keys and store them with `wrangler secret put`:
   - `SESSION_SIGNING_KEY` and `SESSION_SIGNING_KEY_ID`
   - `AUDIT_HASH_KEY` and `AUDIT_HASH_KEY_ID`
   - `IDENTIFIER_HASH_KEY`
   - `APPLICATION_ORIGIN`, set to the `workers.dev` URL. If your account has no `workers.dev` subdomain yet, the first deploy will ask you to register one.
6. **Deploy:** run `npm run ui:build && npx wrangler deploy`.
7. **Checks:**
   - The live URL serves over HTTPS.
   - A good sign-in works.
   - A wrong password and an unknown email each give the same refusal.
   - A suspended employee is refused.

If you pick A, the seed change gets a contract and a unit test and goes through the gate before anything is deployed. I'll ask before committing it.

## 2. A gap the spec already covers, but the code and api.md don't

S05g requires a WebSocket connection from another website to get 403. But api.md A5, and `originAllowed` in the code, only check `Origin` on requests that aren't GETs, and a WebSocket upgrade is a GET. So that refusal isn't enforced today; the only protection is the SameSite cookie.

- **D11, message size limit (recommended):** a chat message over 16 KiB, the same limit as HTTP bodies, is refused before the model is called. This caps abuse and model cost. It adds scenario S05l.

  - The verification tests check it by counting statements.

### Assistant

Good questions. The short version: the Origin check isn't trying to stop someone who forges headers. It stops a page that hijacks a browser which is already signed in. And the agent tools don't call the HTTP API; they call the same use cases inside the Worker.

## S05g: can `Origin` be faked?

Yes. Anyone using `curl` or a script can send any `Origin` they like, and the server can't tell. The check doesn't defend against that person, and it doesn't need to: they don't have a session cookie, so they get 401 anyway.

The attack it does defend against is this. A CSR who is signed in visits `attacker.example`, and that page quietly opens a connection to our site from the CSR's browser. This is called cross-site request forgery, or cross-site WebSocket hijacking for WebSockets.

- **The browser sends the cookie for them.** Our credential is an HttpOnly cookie, and the browser attaches it to requests on its own. The attacking page doesn't need to steal it.
- **The browser sets `Origin` itself.** A page's scripts can't change it, so the hijacked request truthfully says `Origin: https://attacker.example`. Checking it shuts the attack down.

So the two cases split cleanly:

| Who | Can fake `Origin`? | Has the CSR's cookie? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Attacker's page, running in the CSR's browser | no (the browser sets it) | the browser sends it for them |
| Attacker with `curl` | yes | no |

`SameSite=Strict` already stops the browser from sending the cookie on cross-site requests, WebSocket handshakes included. `workers.dev` is on the Public Suffix List, so even other `*.workers.dev` sites count as cross-site. The Origin check is a second layer, for browser edge cases and for any later change to the cookie settings. That's why I recommended D9.

**On Bearer and DPoP:** they solve the other problem.

- **Bearer token in a header:** the browser never attaches it on its own, so cross-site forgery isn't a risk. The risk is the token being stolen and replayed. DPoP fixes that by tying the token to a key the client holds.
- **Our cookie:** it's still a bearer credential, so whoever has it can use it. We reduce the theft risk differently: page scripts can't read it (HttpOnly), it only travels over HTTPS (Secure), and it lasts 30 minutes.

Tying the session to a key, like DPoP or Device Bound Session Credentials, is the production upgrade. It isn't something the Origin check is meant to replace.

## S05j: how long does a WebSocket connection last?

You've understood it correctly.

1. **Opening:** a WebSocket starts as one HTTP request, the upgrade, and the cookie is sent only then. The Worker checks that token and learns its expiry time.
2. **While it's open:** messages flow both ways with no headers and no cookie. Renewing the cookie updates the browser's copy, but the open connection never sees it.
3. **The server enforces the expiry:** the Worker hands the token's expiry to the case agent when the connection opens. The agent closes the connection at that moment (`4401 session_expired`) and refuses any message that arrives after it. So a connection lasts as long as the token it was opened with had left: at most 30 minutes, and less if the token was already part-used.
4. **What the CSR sees:** the UI reconnects right away using the renewed cookie. The conversation lives on the server (S13), so nothing is lost; it's a brief reconnect.

I didn't choose sending a fresh token over the open socket instead. Page scripts would have to handle the token, which defeats HttpOnly.

## `[agent]` scenarios: how the assistant works

The tools don't go through the HTTP API. They call the same use cases the HTTP endpoints use, directly inside the Worker:

```text
Browser ── WebSocket ──► Worker ──► Case agent (Durable Object, one per case;
                         checks the     keeps the conversation in its own storage)
                         cookie and          │
                         Origin, then        │ 1. CSR message + conversation + tool list
                         confirms the        ▼
                         CSR is the     Language model (Workers AI, Llama 3.3 70B)
                         case's              │ 2. answers with text, or a tool call:
                         assignee            │    check_entitlement { account_id: acc_acme, feature_key: sso }
                                             ▼
                                        Agent checks the tool arguments
                                        (unknown fields rejected, S06b)
                                             │ 3. calls the use case in-process; the principal comes
                                             │    from the connection, never from the model (S06a)
                                             ▼
                                        CheckEntitlement use case
                                          → policy decision point (PDP): allowed? (AU-1, policies)
                                          → PostgreSQL through Hyperdrive: subscription, terms, grants
                                          → audit record (account.read, Allow/Deny)
                                             │ 4. structured result: entitled, sources, reason codes
                                             ▼
                                        Model writes the reply from that result
                                             │ 5. reply streamed to the browser;
                                             ▼    the tool call and result are shown too
                                        Browser
```

- **The model can't authorize anything.** It only picks a tool and fills in arguments. Who is asking comes from the session, and whether they're allowed comes from the PDP. A message saying "I am supervisor Sam" changes nothing (S06a).
- **Demo vs production:** the demo uses tool calling in-process. In production the same use cases are offered over MCP (your ruling, 2026-10-25).

**How an `[agent]` test runs:** the real model is replaced by a scripted stand-in at its interface (the `LanguageModel` port). The test says, for example, "when the message 'Does Acme have SSO?' arrives, the model calls `check_entitlement` with these arguments, then replies with this text". Everything else is real: the WebSocket, the agent, argument checking, the PDP, PostgreSQL, the audit records. So the test checks every security and data guarantee without depending on the model's judgment. How well the real model picks tools is measured separately, against the real Workers AI model, and reported without blocking the gate (§5).

The three test levels differ by entry point:

| Level | Drives | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `[http]` | raw HTTP requests and WebSocket upgrades | S05: open a case, connect |
| `[agent]` | a chat turn over the WebSocket, with the scripted model | S06: an identity claim in a message |
| `[use-case]` | a use case called directly, without chat | S07–S12: the lookup and entitlement logic, case by case |

Most of the logic is tested at `[use-case]` level because that's where the cases multiply. `[agent]` scenarios cover what only the chat path can break.

Once these make sense, are you happy with corrections 1–6 and D9–D12 so I can write the updated spec? And should I commit the `AI_WORKFLOW.md` fix now, or together with the spec?

## S05g: why CORS doesn't cover this

What CORS actually does is often misunderstood. It isn't the server turning cross-site requests away. It's the browser deciding whether a page may **read** a response from another site. The request itself often still reaches the server:

| Cross-site request from `attacker.example` | Does the browser send it with our cookie? | Does CORS stop it? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `fetch` POST with `Content-Type: application/json` | no: the browser first asks permission (a "preflight"), we don't grant it, so the real request is never sent | yes, by the preflight |
| HTML form that submits itself (`text/plain` or form-encoded) | **yes**: "simple" requests skip the preflight; the browser only hides the response | **no**: the server has already acted |
| `new WebSocket("wss://our-site/agents/case-agent/…")` | **yes**: WebSockets aren't covered by CORS at all; no preflight | **no** |

So it isn't an iframe. It's ordinary script on the attacker's page: `new WebSocket(...)`. The browser opens the connection to our site and would attach the CSR's cookie. The attacker's script could then send chat messages as that CSR and read the replies. Iframes are a separate attack, clickjacking, and our `frame-ancestors 'none'` header already blocks those.

So yes, it's a second layer, but the first layer is `SameSite=Strict`, not CORS:

1. **SameSite=Strict:** the browser doesn't send the cookie on a cross-site handshake, so the connection arrives without a session and gets 401.
2. **Origin check:** if the cookie gets through anyway, the browser still truthfully labels where the connection came from, and the server refuses it with 403. The cookie could get through because of a browser bug, an older browser, or a later change to the cookie settings.

For WebSockets, the server's own check is the only layer that doesn't depend on cookie behavior. That's the reason for D9.

## S05j: case agents and connections

One correction to your assumption: there's one agent **per case**, not per connection. A case agent is a Durable Object named by its case id, so every connection to `cse_alex_1` reaches the same instance. It outlives connections:

- **Between connections:** it keeps the conversation in its own storage and sleeps while nobody is connected. It costs nothing while asleep.
- **Several connections:** if the CSR opens two tabs, one agent serves both. Each connection keeps its own session details on the server: the account it acts for and the token's expiry. The client can never write these (a security rule from the developer guide).
- **Reconnecting** reaches the same agent with the same history. That's S13: nothing is re-run, and the model isn't called.

```text
Browser                 Worker                       Case agent cse_alex_1        PostgreSQL
   │                       │                          (Durable Object)                │
   │ 1. GET /agents/case-agent/cse_alex_1             │                               │
   │    Upgrade: websocket, Origin, Cookie T (exp 15:20)                              │
   │──────────────────────►│                          │                               │
   │                       │ 2. Origin matches? (D9)  │                               │
   │                       │    T valid? principal active? ─────────────────────────►│
   │                       │    assignee of cse_alex_1? (D1, else 404) ─────────────►│
   │                       │ 3. hands over the upgrade with the verified session:     │
   │                       │    principal iac_alex, expires 15:20                     │
   │                       │─────────────────────────►│ stores them with this         │
   │                       │                          │ connection; sets a timer      │
   │◄───────────────────── 101 Switching Protocols ───│ for 15:20                     │
   │                       │                          │                               │
   │ 4. message "Does Acme have SSO?" (no headers, no cookie)                         │
   │─────────────────────────────────────────────────►│ expired? principal still      │
   │                       │                          │ active? (D10) ───────────────►│
   │                       │                          │ model → tool → use case ─────►│
   │◄──────────────────────────── streamed reply ─────│                               │
   │                       │                          │                               │
   │ 5. 15:10 POST /api/authentication/session/renewal (Cookie T)                     │
   │──────────────────────►│ new cookie T2 (exp 15:40)│                               │
   │◄──────────────────────│  the open connection never sees it                       │
   │                       │                          │                               │
   │                       │                     15:20 timer fires                    │
   │◄───────────── close 4401 session_expired ────────│ conversation stays stored     │
   │                       │                          │                               │
   │ 6. UI reconnects at once: same as step 1, with Cookie T2 (exp 15:40)             │
   │──────────────────────►│ ... ────────────────────►│ same agent, same history (S13)│
```

Step 3 is the one that matters for security. The session details come from the server's own check. The browser can't supply or change them, and neither can the model. Exactly how they're handed over (for example, headers the Worker sets and strips from client input, or the agent re-checking the cookie itself) is a choice for the build plan. The spec only requires the result.

Agreed on the alternative design. Handling the token in the application would let a connection be refreshed in place, but then page scripts would handle the token, which reopens the theft risk HttpOnly closes. Keeping the cookie approach is the right call for the demo.

And yes, a tool is a use case the model may ask to run. The model chooses which one and the arguments; the server decides who is asking and whether they're allowed.

## How several connections reach the same case agent

An agent never handles more than one case. It isn't shared across cases. Each case has its own agent, and the case id is the agent's address: `/agents/case-agent/cse_alex_1` always reaches the agent for `cse_alex_1`, and `cse_alex_2` reaches a different one. "Several connections" just means the same case open more than once at the same time:

```text
Alex, tab 1 ──┐
              ├──► case agent cse_alex_1   (one conversation, two live connections)
Alex, tab 2 ──┘
Alex, tab 3 ─────► case agent cse_alex_2   (another case, another agent)
```

Each tab opens its own WebSocket. Both go to the same agent because they name the same case. The agent keeps the session details separately for each connection, and it sends replies to every connection so both tabs show the same conversation. Since only the case's assignee can connect (D1), every connection to one case is the same CSR.

## Do we need our own message protocol?

Mostly no. The Agents SDK comes with a chat protocol: user messages, cancel, streamed replies, and tool calls with their results. We define only our additions, all listed in api.md §8:
- the custom cards and events later features use (`subject_card`, `change_request_card`, `case_event`)
- the close codes

For this feature the additions are just three close codes: `4401 session_expired`, `4401 invalid_session` (D10), and `1009 message_too_big` (D11, which uses the standard WebSocket code for an oversized message). The spec also says that any client frame that tries to write agent state is rejected.

## Two problems that need your call

**1. D12 can't hold as I worded it.** You approved "refusals that must look identical also run the same statements". But in S07d, an employee without `account.read` gets an audit record (Deny), while "nothing matches" writes none, because there's no account to decide about. Writing an audit record is a database write, so the two refusals take different amounts of work. The difference in timing could tell Drew that an account exists.
- **A (recommended):** a lookup that matches nothing also writes one audit record: `account.read`, no subject, outcome `Failed`, with a new reason code `account.no-match`. Both refusals then do the same work, and failed lookups also show up in the audit trail. That's useful anyway, for spotting someone probing for accounts. This changes S07d Case B and adds a reason code.
- **B:** narrow D12 to refusals whose audit records match, and accept the timing difference for insiders who lack `account.read`. That weakens your rule against revealing whether something exists, so I don't recommend it.

**2. S05f needs a security-event cause, and the code has none for it.** Connecting to a case that isn't yours, or that doesn't exist, writes one security-event entry, but none of the existing causes fits.
- **Recommended:** two new names, `CaseNotFound` and `CaseNotAssigned`. The log is internal, so it can tell them apart, and that helps spot someone guessing case ids. The caller still sees the same 404.
- **Alternative:** one name, `CaseUnavailable`, matching the existing close code `4404 case_unavailable`.

Once you've ruled on these two, I'll finish the spec and send it back for full approval.

## Point 1: what if someone floods us with "nothing matches" lookups?

Flooding is a real risk, but option A doesn't create it, and something cheaper than the audit write limits it.

**It's already possible today.** Under the current spec, every lookup that finds something already writes one audit record per candidate, whether Allow or Deny. Drew asking about accounts 1,000 times already writes 1,000 Deny records. Option A only makes "nothing matches" behave like the cases that already write.

**It's expensive for the attacker before it costs us anything.** Nobody can call `find_customer_account` directly. The only way in is:
1. sign in as an employee;
2. open a case they're assigned to;
3. send a chat message;
4. wait for the model to decide to call the tool.

Every lookup costs a full model call, which is slow and, on Workers AI, limited and paid for. One message produces at most a few lookups. The model is the bottleneck, not the database.

**The real weak point is elsewhere.** Audit writes go one at a time, because each record extends a hash chain under a lock. A flood of any audited action, not just lookups, would make every employee's audited actions slower. That means the protection should limit messages per employee, not leave lookups unaudited.

**And the flood would be visible.** With option A, a stream of no-match records from one employee is exactly the evidence of probing that an investigator wants. Under option B the probing leaves no trace.

**Recommendation:** option A, plus one new decision.

- **D13, a message rate limit for each employee:** at most 20 chat messages per minute per employee, counted across all their connections. A message over the limit is refused before the principal is re-loaded or the model is called. The connection closes with `4429 rate_limited`, and the UI waits and reconnects. That caps how much model work and how many audit writes one employee can cause, whatever the messages say. Production-readiness already lists per-employee rate limits; this brings a simple version into the demo. It adds one scenario, S05m, with a scripted burst of 21 messages.

The numbers are your call: 20 per minute fits a CSR typing, with room to spare. The other choice is whether the 21st message should close the connection (simplest, as above) or get a refusal frame while the connection stays open. That's friendlier, but it needs a custom frame type, which the protocol doesn't have yet.

Do you accept A with D13 as described (20 per minute, close with `4429`)? Then I'll finish the spec: S07d Case B, the new reason code `account.no-match`, D12 reworded, and S05m. It then comes back to you for full approval.

The lookup runs in Support, and `account.` codes describe an account's state, such as a suspension. Please confirm the name.

## D13: who enforces the rate limit?

The server does, inside the case agent, before it does anything else with a message. Every message has to pass through the agent, so the browser can't get around it. The agent asks a **rate limiter** port, "may iac_alex send another message?", and only then re-loads the principal or calls the model. The open question is which adapter sits behind that port:

| Adapter | How it works | Exact? | Testable with our clock? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **One Durable Object per employee (recommended)** | One counter per employee, so all their connections and cases share it | yes: one counter, one place | yes: it uses our `Clock` port |
| Cloudflare's Rate Limiting binding | a built-in counter keyed by employee | approximate: counted per Cloudflare location | no: it uses Cloudflare's clock, so tests can't control it |
| The case agent alone | counts inside each case | per case, not per employee: 10 open cases would get 10× the limit | yes |

Cloudflare's rate-limiting rules (WAF) don't fit. They count HTTP requests, not WebSocket messages, and they aren't available on `workers.dev`.

I recommend the Durable Object. It's exact, it uses Durable Objects, which we already run, and the scripted 21-message test (S05m) gives the same result every run. Because the limiter is a port, switching to Cloudflare's binding later means changing only the adapter and the composition root. Production-readiness already lists sign-in lockout and rate limiting as gaps; the same port could guard sign-in later.

## New evidence: D12 needs a better shape

While applying A, I found that matching the amount of work still doesn't hide everything. Say Drew, who has no `account.read`, looks up "acme". Today the spec decides once per matching account, so Drew gets one Deny record per match: two for "acme", one for a single match, and one no-match record for nothing. The number of database writes, and so the response time, still reveals how many accounts matched.

**The fix:** refuse an employee without `account.read` before any customer data is read. That means one decision, one audit record, and no search at all, so nothing in the response depends on what exists.

- **Why it's safe:** without the permission, the answer is Deny whatever the account is (deny by default; policies only add requirements).
- **Employees with `account.read`** keep the per-account decisions. Learning that an account exists is allowed for them (AU-6).
- **D12 becomes:** "a principal without `account.read` is refused before any customer data is read". This replaces "the same statements". The tests check it by confirming that no customer table is queried.
- **Scenarios that change:**
  - S07d Case A: Drew's record names no account.
  - S08c Case A and S08e: a check or list by Drew records the account id he asked for, whether it exists or not. Audit records may name an account that doesn't exist, so the record is the same in both cases.
- **D2 is narrowed:** lookups decide once per matching account only for employees who hold `account.read`.

Three things to decide:
1. **D13:** a Durable Object per employee, 20 messages a minute, close with `4429`?
2. **D12:** refuse before reading customer data, with the D2 change above?
3. **Reason code:** `support.no-matching-account`?

**Eight phases.** The verification tests are written after P1 and must all fail first (the RED gate):

| # | Phase | Turns green |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P1 | Database tables, seed data, test harness, then the verification tests | — |
| P2 | Product and Entitlement: effective entitlements in one pure function | — |
| P3 | Policy: CEL evaluation, policy-set version, registration and activation | — |
| P4 | Authorization: the decision chain, plus the use-case template (permission check first per D12, then decide, run, audit) | — |
| P5 | The four read use cases and `POST /api/cases` | S05a–d, S07–S12 |
| P6 | Case agent, tools, message checks, rate limiter, model adapter | S05e–m, S06, S13 |
| P7 | Chat UI | — |
| P8 | End-to-end test, deploy, live-model evaluation | — |

**New names to approve** (§9): message rate limiter, connection session, permission gate, policy expression evaluator, scripted language model.

| # | Question | Recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |

| Q4 | Fixture dates on the live demo: the spec's subscription dates are fixed around 2026-10-22, so on the live site some will gradually lapse. | Add an option to shift all fixture dates to the seeding day when seeding the deployed database. | Q5 | One feature, or split after P5? | One feature, as approved. P5 is a natural checkpoint, with most scenarios green. |

- **Size:** this feature is several times larger than sign-in.

**One security finding from K2:** the SDK's chat agent takes the **entire message history from the browser** on every message. Browsers can also send frames that clear the chat or submit tool results. Left as is, a page could forge earlier assistant replies or tool results. Our case agent will keep the conversation on the server: it takes only the newest user message's text and refuses every other client frame. That's already what the spec's §3 requires ("a client frame that writes agent state is rejected"), so the spec doesn't change. P6 will test it explicitly.

Then I write the verification tests from the spec and run the RED gate, where every test must fail for the reason the spec predicts. I'll report the RED gate result to you before building P2. Shall I go ahead?

## What P1 delivered

- **Database:** seven migrations (customers, customer accounts, catalog, feature permissions and account roles, entitlements, policies, cases). The layer test confirms every foreign key points down the layer order.
- **Seed data:**
  - the catalog, the customer roles, and every customer, membership, subscription, add-on, grant and hold from spec §2;
  - the date-shift option (`SEED_FIXTURE_DAY`), so the live database matches the spec's dates;
  - I wrote the shift before its tests, so I broke it on purpose twice; both breaks were caught.
- **Three test harnesses:**
  - use cases, called through a new `withUseCases` entry point;
  - the plain-class case agent, with the scripted model and a recording connection;
  - the real Worker running locally in the Workers runtime, for S05e and S13, which need a real `101` handshake that Node can't produce.
- **Stubs:** the five Support use cases and the case agent exist with their final interfaces, but do nothing yet.
- **Checks:** every other test still passes (123); types, layering and lint are clean.

## Why the 72 fail

Each fails on an assertion, for the expected reason:
- the new routes don't exist, so the tests get 404 where they expect 201, 401, 403 or 101;
- the stub use cases answer `NotFound` where a result is expected;
- no audit records or security events are written;
- the stub agent never closes a connection or calls a tool.

None fails because a module is missing.

## Decisions for you

| # | Item | Recommendation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **S05c passes already.** The origin check from sign-in runs before routing on every non-GET request, so opening a case from another site is refused before `/api/cases` even exists. | Keep it as a guard for the new endpoint, and prove it can fail by breaking the global check during the build. |
| 2 | **S08e case C passes already.** Checking an account that doesn't exist should return `NotFound` with no audit record, and that happens to be the stub's default. | Keep it, and prove it can fail by breaking it once the real check exists. |
| 3 | **S05j spec defect.** It says `audit_records: none`, but its own renewal step writes a `session.renew` record, as sign-in requires. | Amend the line to "only the renewal's `session.renew`". The test already asserts that. |
| 4 | **S05l and S05m say "principal not re-loaded",** which can't be observed from outside the agent. | Cover the check order with a unit test in P6; the verification tests keep the observable parts (the close, the model calls, the audit). |
| 5 | **S09a lists the covering term** for cases D, E and G only, though a check returns it for every entitled feature. | Amend the spec to state it in every entitled case, so the tests assert it everywhere. The alternative is to keep testing only what each case states. |

One small inconsistency, recorded and to be fixed in P3: the support diagram uses the prefix `psa_` for policy activations, but the id register says `act_`. I'm following the register.

Do you accept 1–5? And may I commit P1 with the RED tests? Once committed, the tests become immutable; changing them later needs your agreement. Then I'll start P2 (products and entitlements).

## What was built

- **The case agent:**
  - **Message checks:** each message is checked in the spec's fixed order (size D11, rate D13, token expiry D6, principal D10) before the model sees it. A unit test pins that order, which closes discovery 14.
  - **Tools and model loop:** the four tools refuse unknown arguments. The principal and case come from the connection, never from the model, and one message gets at most five model steps.
  - **Server-side conversation:** browser-supplied history and every other client frame (clear chat, tool results, tool approvals) change nothing.
- **Rate limiter:** one sliding-window rule (20 messages per minute per employee), shared by the in-memory and Durable Object adapters, both held to one contract test suite.
- **Workers AI adapter:** tested with the AI SDK's own mock model; the live model is never called in tests.
- **Durable Objects:**
  - each connection's session lives in a server-side attachment on its WebSocket, which survives hibernation;
  - the alarm closes connections when their tokens expire;
  - the Worker forwards upgrades with headers it built itself. A new test in the Workers runtime confirms that a browser's forged `X-Connection-Session` header is ignored.
- **Proofs:** I broke six guarantees on purpose, and a test caught every break: the size limit, strict arguments, the principal re-check, forged headers, the restored conversation, and the check order.

## Test infrastructure findings

Wrangler's local test harness was misleading the S05e and S13 tests (discovery 22):
- **Its local secrets file overrode the test Worker's own variables.** The test Worker now uses `TEST_`-prefixed settings, so local secrets can never stand in for them.
- **It rewrote requests:** `https` became `http` in the URL and `Origin`, and WebSocket upgrades got its own handshake key. Upgrades now go over a raw TCP connection, so S05e checks the exact handshake RFC 6455 specifies.

## 20: Why a plain Durable Object rather than the SDK's `AIChatAgent`?

**What `AIChatAgent` would give us:**
- the chat protocol handled for us, with stored messages;
- **resumable streams:** a dropped connection picks up a reply that's still streaming;
- **live multi-tab updates:** every open tab sees each new message as it happens;
- drop-in compatibility with the SDK's React hook (`useAgentChat`);
- client-side tools and tool approvals, MCP, scheduling and built-in tracing.

**Why it doesn't fit us:**

| Concern | `AIChatAgent` | Plain Durable Object (chosen) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Who owns the conversation | The browser sends the **whole history** with every message, and the agent stores it (spike K2). A page can forge earlier assistant replies or tool results. | Only the server stores it; the browser contributes one message's text. |
| Other client frames | Clearing the chat, submitting tool results, approving tools: all accepted by default, and each needs overriding and testing. | Ignored by construction: there's one accepted frame type. |
| Our rules | The developer guide says the client must never write the agent's state, so we'd be switching off the SDK's main features one by one. | Satisfied by design. |
| Dependencies | The `agents` and `@cloudflare/ai-chat` packages, plus three required MCP packages we don't use. During the spike, two of these packages had just been renamed or moved. | None of them on the server; the AI SDK only, inside one adapter. |
| Testing | Tied to the SDK's runtime classes. | The agent is a plain class tested in Node (12 unit tests, 7 scenarios); the Durable Object is a thin wrapper. |

**What we give up:**
1. **Resumable streams.** If a connection drops mid-reply, the partial reply isn't resumed. The conversation is saved once the turn ends, so the next reconnect shows the full reply, but not live.
2. **Live updates across tabs.** A second tab on the same case sees new messages only when it reconnects. That's easy to add later by broadcasting the turn to every open connection.
3. **Drop-in UI hook.** The SDK's `useAgentChat` may expect frames we don't send. P7 will either check compatibility or use a small client of our own, about 100 lines.
4. **We maintain the protocol code** (about 200 lines) and must follow the SDK's frame format if we want to stay compatible with its clients.

**Recommendation:** accept. The trade is all convenience for security, and your rule is security over speed. If we later want the SDK's routing or scheduling, we can use its base `Agent` class, which doesn't take the client's history, and keep our own message handling.

## 23: What do we lose by not testing the Durable Object with doubles?

Real costs, and checking them exposed two gaps in what I just built:

1. **Failure paths are hard to reach.** With doubles, it's easy to make the database, the model or storage fail. With the real stack, it isn't. **Gap found:** if the model call or a use case throws during a turn, the agent sends no error and never sends the final `done` frame, so the browser waits forever. No spec scenario covers it, and no test caught it.
2. **The Durable Object rate limiter was never held to the limiter's contract tests.** S05m used the in-memory limiter. The production limiter shares the same sliding-window function, so the counting rule is tested, but its storage round-trip isn't. **Gap found.**
3. **Some lifecycle behavior can't be forced in the local harness:** waking from hibernation (re-attaching connections), rescheduling the alarm after a close, and closing during a turn. They're reviewed but not tested.
4. **Slower tests.** Each runtime test starts a local Workers runtime and a fresh database, about half a second each, versus milliseconds. So we run few cases there.
5. **Failures are less local.** A failure can come from the harness, the runtime, the Durable Object, the application or the database. We already hit three harness quirks (discovery 22).
6. **Dependence on the local tools,** whose behavior changes between versions.

**What the real stack gives us in return:** it caught real issues doubles would have hidden. That includes the exact handshake (S05e), the restored-conversation timing (S13), and, most importantly, it proved the forged session header is refused end to end.

**Recommendation:** keep the end-to-end tests as the main proof, and close the two gaps inside this feature. Both are small:
- **The error path:** a turn that fails sends an error chunk and the final `done` frame, with a generic message to the browser and the details only in the logs. That gets a unit test with a failing model and a failing tool, then a test in the runtime.
- **The Durable Object limiter's contract:** run the limiter's contract suite against the real Durable Object limiter through a test-only route in the Worker under test.

The lifecycle cases in point 3 would be recorded as accepted risks in production-readiness, because the local harness can't force hibernation.

## Decisions

1. Do you accept 20 as described?
2. Should I close the two gaps from 23 before committing P6?

## 20: Which parts of the Agents SDK each option uses

| SDK part | What it does | A: subclass `AIChatAgent` | B: plain Durable Object (built) | C: extend the SDK's base `Agent` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `routeAgentRequest` | routes `/agents/<class>/<name>` to the right Durable Object | ✓ | — (our Worker routes, after its own checks) | ✓ (after our checks) |
| `Agent` base class | Durable Object lifecycle, connection objects with per-connection state, hibernation, `schedule()`, SQL helper | ✓ | — (we use the runtime's Durable Object and WebSocket APIs directly) | ✓ |
| `Agent` state sync | shared state the **browser can write** (`setState`) | ✓, must be blocked | — | ✓, must be blocked |
| `AIChatAgent` | chat protocol, stored messages, resumable streams, live multi-tab updates, tool continuations | ✓ (**takes the browser's history**) | — (our agent speaks the same frame format) | — (our agent handles the messages) |
| Required MCP packages | peers of `agents`, unused by us | 3 packages | none | 3 packages |
| AI SDK (`ai`, `workers-ai-provider`) | calls the model | ✓ (inside `onChatMessage`) | ✓ (in one adapter only) | ✓ (in one adapter only) |
| Client hooks: `useAgent`, `useAgentChat` | browser connection with reconnects; chat state | ✓ | possibly `useAgent`, or our own client (P7 decides) | ✓ |

**In plain terms:**
- **A** uses nearly all of the SDK, and we'd spend effort switching off what conflicts with our rules.
- **B** uses none of it on the server. The Durable Object pieces A or C would provide are the ones I already wrote: routing, per-connection session, alarms and hibernation, together about 200 lines in `durable-objects.ts`.
- **C** is in between. It brings the SDK's routing, connection handling and scheduling, but still needs the MCP packages, and its browser-writable state must be blocked.

**Recommendation: still B.** Nothing on the server comes from the SDK, so none of its defaults can weaken our rules. If later features want `schedule()` or the SDK's connection objects, moving to C is contained: only the Durable Object wrapper changes, and the case agent itself doesn't.

## 23: Both kinds of test? Yes. That's what I recommend.

Real end-to-end tests and tests with doubles answer different questions, so I'd use three layers:

| Layer | Runs in | Doubles of | Covers |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Unit** | Node, milliseconds | the agent's ports (model, rate limiter, conversation store, use cases), and a **fake Durable Object runtime** (state, storage, WebSockets). It's possible because `durable-objects.ts` already declares the small slice of the runtime API it uses. | the error paths (a failing model, tool or store must still send an error and `done`); waking from hibernation and re-attaching connections; rescheduling the alarm after a close or an expiry; closing during a turn |
| **Contract** | Node, plus the real runtime | storage only, in Node | the rate limiter's contract suite against the in-memory limiter, the Durable Object limiter on fake storage, **and** the real Durable Object limiter in the runtime (through a test-only route) |
| **End to end** | the real Workers runtime and the real database | only the scripted model | the handshake (S05e), reconnect (S13), forged session header (done), and one error path: a model failure shows the browser an error and ends the turn |

**Risk of the fakes:** a fake runtime could behave differently from the real one. The end-to-end layer is what catches that, so each lifecycle area has at least one real test where the harness allows. Hibernation can't be forced locally, so it stays unit-tested only; I'll list it in production-readiness.

**Code change needed:** the turn's error handling. A failing turn sends an error chunk with a generic message, then the final `done` frame; the details go only to the logs, with the trace id.

**Cost:** about a dozen unit tests, the contract suite run against two more limiters, and two runtime tests.

## Decisions

1. **Discovery 20:** B, as built?
2. **Discovery 23:** the three layers above, plus the error-handling fix, before committing P6?

That changes the trade-off, and I should have asked about the goal behind the stack choice. The proposal names `AIChatAgent` as the agent's stack, and I didn't weigh its value for your application. First your question, then a revised recommendation.

## With B, do we define the message structures?

There are two different message formats:

| Messages | Option B (built) | With the SDK (`AIChatAgent`) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **To Llama** (prompt, tool definitions, tool calls) | We don't define Llama's format. The AI SDK and `workers-ai-provider` handle it, same as with the SDK. We do define our own small port types (`ConversationMessage`, `ModelStep`), and an adapter translates them to the AI SDK's. | Same AI SDK and provider. `streamText` takes the SDK's message types directly, so there are no port types of our own. |
| **To the browser** (chat protocol) | **Yes, we reimplement it.** We hand-build the SDK's frames (`cf_agent_use_chat_request` in; `cf_agent_use_chat_response` with streamed chunks out; `cf_agent_chat_messages` on reconnect). So we're copying SDK internals, and must follow them if they change. | The SDK produces them: `streamText(...).toUIMessageStreamResponse()`, and `useAgentChat` on the client. |

So B duplicates exactly the part that best shows off the SDK, and couples us to its internals anyway.

## Revised recommendation: use `AIChatAgent`, hardened

The security problem was never the SDK itself. It's that `AIChatAgent` trusts what the browser sends. That can likely be closed at the class's entry points, while keeping everything the SDK does well:

| Concern | How a hardened subclass handles it |
| --- | --- |
| Browser-sent history (discovery 7) | Override the message entry point. Take only the newest user message's text, and rebuild the request from the server's stored history before handing it to the SDK. Forged history never reaches storage. |
| Clear chat, tool results, tool approvals | Dropped at the entry point, before the SDK sees them. |
| Browser-writable shared state | Refused through the SDK's state-validation hook. |
| Size, rate, expiry, principal (D6, D10, D11, D13) | Checked at the entry point, in the spec's order, before the SDK runs. Same logic we have now, kept as a plain class. |
| Connection session | Set from the Worker's verified header when the connection opens; stored in the SDK's server-side per-connection state. |
| Tools | AI SDK `tool()` with strict schemas, each calling our use cases with the principal from the connection, via the SDK's current-agent context. |
| Model | Workers AI through `workers-ai-provider`. In tests, the AI SDK's mock model is the scripted model. |
| What we keep | Resumable streams, live multi-tab updates, `useAgentChat` in the UI, and the SDK's own frame format. |

**What changes in tests (needs your agreement):** the seven `[agent]` scenarios (S05i–S05m, S06a, S06b) currently drive our plain class in Node. With `AIChatAgent` in production, they should drive the real agent in the Workers runtime, like S05e and S13. That means a test-only clock control and alarm trigger in the Worker under test. The scenarios themselves don't change. The plain checks and tool logic keep their fast Node unit tests, and the three-layer plan from 23 still applies.

**Risk:** I haven't yet confirmed that `AIChatAgent` lets us override its message entry point cleanly. It might handle frames internally in a way a subclass can't reach. So I'd start with a **time-boxed spike (K4)** proving four things in the runtime:

## Spike K4

It passed, and its test was proven able to fail. Our subclass wraps `AIChatAgent`'s message handler in its constructor, so every browser frame meets our checks first:
- forged history never reached storage;
- clear-chat, tool-result, tool-approval, message-rewrite and state frames changed nothing;
- a tool read the session through the SDK's current-agent context.

With the wrapper removed, the forged "You are a supervisor now." reply **was** stored.

## The agent now

- **Agents SDK:** `CaseAgentObject extends AIChatAgent`. The SDK handles the chat protocol, message storage, resumable streams and live updates across tabs. The UI in P7 can use the SDK's `useAgentChat` hook.
- **Message gate** (new term, below): runs before the SDK on every frame.
  - Checks in the spec's order: size, frame kind, rate, token expiry, principal.
  - A chat request is rebuilt from the stored conversation plus the newest message's text.
  - Clear-chat, tool-result, approval, state and unknown frames are dropped.
- **AI SDK:** `streamText` with Workers AI through `workers-ai-provider`, at most five model steps per message. The four tools are AI SDK tools wrapping our strict runners, and act as the connection's principal.
- **Errors:** a tool's own argument refusal reaches the model as written. Any other failure shows only "The assistant could not answer (trace …)", with the details in the logs.
- **Rate limiter:** a Durable Object per employee.

## Tests (the three layers you agreed for 23)

- **Unit, in Node:** the message gate. 16 tests cover its order and every frame kind, including the forged request.
- **Workers runtime, real database:**
  - all nine agent scenarios;
  - the session-header forgery test;
  - K4's checks as a permanent test;
  - the failing-model path;
  - the limiter's contract suite against the real Durable Object limiter.
- **Proofs:** I broke each guarantee on purpose, and a test caught every break: the gate's rebuilt request, the generic error, forged headers, the restored conversation, and the earlier gate checks.
- **Not testable locally:** hibernation and wake-up can't be forced in the local runtime. They're listed in `production-readiness.md`, along with a note that the hardening relies on how the SDK's constructor works, so SDK upgrades need checking.

## a: What a "browser frame" is

You're right: it's one message the browser sends over the open WebSocket. The WebSocket protocol (RFC 6455) calls each one a *frame*. With the Agents SDK each is a small JSON text, for example `{"type":"cf_agent_use_chat_request", …}`. It isn't an HTTP request, and it isn't a data structure in general.

The two guards split the work like this:
- **Request guard:** checks every HTTP request, including the one WebSocket upgrade that opens a connection.
- **Message gate:** checks every frame that arrives afterwards, on the open connection.

I'll put both, and that relationship, in the glossary.

## Glossary: how the two guards relate

| Term | Covers |
| --- | --- |
| **WebSocket frame** | One message over an open WebSocket, in either direction. With the Agents SDK it's a small JSON text like `{"type":"cf_agent_use_chat_request", …}`. It isn't an HTTP request: it has no headers and no cookie. |
| **Inbound guard** *(new category, needs your approval)* | A check an inbound adapter runs before any use case is reached. It decides only whether input may come in (origin, session, size, rate, shape), never what a principal may do; that's the authorization decision's job. There are two. |
| **Request guard** | The inbound guard of every HTTP request, including the WebSocket upgrade that opens a case agent connection. Checks: HTTPS redirect, origin, session token, principal loaded fresh. In `http/request-guard.ts`. |
| **Message gate** | The inbound guard of every WebSocket frame a browser sends once the connection is open, before the Agents SDK sees it. Checks: size, kind, rate, token expiry, principal. A chat request is rebuilt; every other frame that could write the conversation, a tool result or the agent's state is dropped. In `agent/message-gate.ts`. |

I also added **WebSocket**, and updated **Case agent** to say it's built on `AIChatAgent` (CM-18), with a link to the alternative.

## Documenting the alternative

- **ADR CM-18** in `tech-docs/adr/common.md`, "The case agent is a hardened `AIChatAgent`". It records:
  - the decision, context and rationale;
  - the consequences, including that SDK upgrades must pass the hardening tests first;
  - the alternatives (plain Durable Object, and the base `Agent` class), linking to the note.
- **Design note** `tech-docs/design/case-agent-without-agents-sdk.md`, the generous version:
  - when the alternative fits;
  - what each SDK part does for us;
  - the alternative's shape and ports;
  - each SDK feature mapped to plain Workers APIs (routing, session attachments, hibernation wake-up, alarms for expiry, storage, inbound and outbound frames, the tool loop);
  - code excerpts from the build we made and then replaced;
  - security, testing, what's given up (and how to get each back), the middle option (base `Agent`), what changes to move between designs, and a side-by-side comparison.
- **Links:** from api.md §8 and from the glossary's case agent entry.
- **Ruling d:** the architect guide now names the AI SDK's model interface as the language-model port.

## What the UI does

| --- | --- |

| `4429 rate_limited` | "Too many messages in the last minute…" |
| `1009 message_too_big` | "That message is too long…" |

Both are doable. Here's how I'd do each.

## Cmd+Enter to send

Easy: **Cmd+Enter** on macOS, **Ctrl+Enter** on Windows and Linux. The browser reports which modifier was held, so one handler covers all three. Plain Enter keeps adding a new line, and the button gets a hint ("Send · ⌘↵" or "Send · Ctrl+↵", depending on the platform).

## A more structured response

Doable, with one design choice that matters: **where the details come from.**

| Option | How | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A. Ask the model to write the structure | Prompt Llama to answer in your layout. | Not recommended. The model can get facts wrong or format them inconsistently, and we'd show its text as if it were data. |
| B. **Build the details from the tool results** | The model writes only the **Result** sentence. The UI builds **Details** from the tools' structured outputs: account, plan, term, entitled or not, and why. | **Recommended.** The details come from the system of record, so they can't be wrong in ways the data isn't. They're also escaped like everything else. |
| C. Ask the model for JSON and render it | The model returns a JSON summary, which we validate and render. | Not recommended. Llama often breaks JSON (we just saw it garble tool arguments), and it's still model-written facts. |

With **B**, your example would render as:

```text
Tools (4)                                    ▸ collapsed; expand for arguments and results
  find_customer_account · done
  check_entitlement · done  (×3)

Result
  Acme Corp has SSO through its Business plan. Acme Labs doesn't: SSO isn't part of its Free plan.

Details
  Acme Corp  (acc_AaD0…dkw)
    Plan               Business
    Term               Payment, ends 2026-11-01
    SSO                ✓ included · source: Business plan
  Acme Labs  (acc_AaD0…J-A)
    Plan               Free
    Term               Free plan, open-ended
    SSO                ✗ not included · lowest plan that includes it: Business
```

**What it needs:**

1. **Richer tool results.** `check_entitlement` currently omits the account's name and plan when the answer is "not entitled", which is why Acme Labs had no plan in your example. The check and list results would add each account's display name, current plan and term. No spec scenario changes; those fields are additions to the results.
2. **A Details view** that turns the tool results of one reply into account cards:
   - check and list results become plan, term and per-feature lines;
   - explanations become "allowed / denied" with each reason in plain words, using the reason register's text;
   - refusals ("not found", "denied by policy") show as such.
3. **Model instructions:** keep the Result to one or two sentences, use account names rather than ids, and write dates without times (`2026-11-01`, not `2026-11-01T00:00:00.000Z`).
4. **Tools section:** collapsed by default, with a count, and calls with identical results shown once. The full arguments and results stay one click away.

**Effort:** small to moderate, all in the tool results and the UI: no change to the use cases' decisions, the policies, or the security path. The Details view gets Node unit tests for turning tool results into account cards.

## Decisions

1. Option **B**, the details built from tool results?
2. For accounts that lack the feature, should Details also show the **remedy** (the lowest plan that includes it)? The tools already compute it for explanations; checks would need it added. My recommendation: yes, it's what a CSR acts on.
3. Show account **names with a shortened id** (as in the sketch), or names only?

P8, the deploy, needs your two decisions first. Here's the plan they slot into.

## P8 plan

1. **Pre-deploy checks:** a dry-run deploy confirms whether the new bindings (two Durable Objects and Workers AI) work with your current login scopes. If a permission is missing, I'll tell you which one, as with Hyperdrive.
2. **Neon database** (decision 1).
3. **Policy-set registration and activation** (decision 2), by a command run at deploy time.
4. **Deploy**, then a live smoke test:
   - sign-in;
   - opening a case;
   - a chat turn with real Workers AI;
   - an Enterprise refusal for Alex;
   - the `http://` redirect.
5. **The spec's report-only live-model evaluation** (§5): four questions, five runs each, reported as pass rates.
6. **The end-of-feature gates:** the GREEN gate on the final commit, then the adversarial review of the whole feature diff by a separate review agent.

## Decision 1: the production database

Neon holds the sign-in data: the employees with their generated passwords, and the audit records from our smoke tests and your sign-ins. The new feature needs new tables plus the catalog, customer and policy data.

| Option | What happens | Trade-off |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **A. Reset and reseed (recommended)** | Drop and recreate the database schema, apply all migrations, and seed everything. Employees get the **same generated passwords** (from `.env.seed-passwords`), so what you shared with reviewers still works. Fixture dates shift so the spec's day (2026-10-22) falls on the deploy day. | **Destructive:** the existing audit records (smoke tests and your sign-ins) are deleted. They're demo data only. The worker login role and the Hyperdrive setup are unaffected. |
| B. Migrate in place and seed only the new data | Keep everything, apply only the new migrations, and add only the missing data. | Needs a second, incremental seeding path that the tests don't cover, so it adds risk for no lasting benefit. |

## Decision 2: who records a policy-set deployment

Registering and activating a policy set records who did it (`registered_by`, `activated_by`), and that must be an account. The seed currently records Sam, a supervisor, which suggests a person deployed the policies.

| Option | Trade-off |
| --- | --- |
| **A. A dedicated system identity, `sys_deployment` (recommended)** | A new seeded system identity with **no roles**, used only to record deployments, in the seed and in production. Honest attribution: an automated deployment, not a person. |
| B. Keep Sam | Simple, but the record says a person did something they didn't do. |
| C. Your own employee account | You'd need an employee account, which the demo doesn't have. |

Option A adds one fixture entry and changes the recorded actor. The seed and the production command would both use it.

Do you approve **1A**, the reset with the same passwords and fixture dates on the deploy day, and **2A**, `sys_deployment`?

Just one question about system identities; those identities are not authenticated right; right now they are just like labels?

### Assistant

Yes. In the demo, system identities aren't authenticated. What each one actually is:

| | Employees (`emp_…`) | System identities (`sys_…`) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credential | a password (`employee_credential`) | **none**: no row to sign in with |
| Can sign in or get a session | yes | no: sign-in only accepts employees |
| How they act | through requests carrying their verified session | through **server-side code the runtime starts** (a scheduled job or a Workflow), never through a request |
| What protects them from impersonation | the session token | **no external path to them**: no request can name a principal (rule `principal-comes-from-session`), so only our own server code can act as a system account |
| Authorization | roles and the decision point | also roles and the decision point: `sys_grant_expiry` and `sys_approval_process` hold `entitlement-maintenance`, and their actions are still decided and audited |

So for the two existing ones, being "the system" isn't just a label. It's trusted because only our server code can start actions as them, and their permissions are still checked and audited. They act in later features (grant expiry, applied approvals).

**`sys_deployment` is purely an attribution label**, as you suspect:
- it has no roles, and never acts through a use case;
- the real authentication for registering a policy set is **whoever holds the database owner's connection string** (`.env.neon`), because the deploy command runs with those credentials.

The record then says "registered by the deployment" rather than a made-up person, which is accurate, but it proves nothing about *who* deployed.

**In production** this gap is real, and it's already listed in production-readiness as "workload credentials for system accounts". Deployments would run from CI with their own workload identity: for example GitHub Actions' signed OIDC token, exchanged for a short-lived credential. They'd register the policy set through an endpoint that checks that credential and maps it to `sys_deployment`. The record would then name an authenticated actor, with the CI run as evidence.

When we proceed, I'd add one line to that production-readiness entry saying that deployments are recorded as `sys_deployment`, but authenticated only by the owner's database credentials.

**The wording of the reply is the weak part.** I logged this as discovery 33 and marked P8 as in progress.
- **JSON instead of an answer:** in 7 of the 20 runs, the forced last step wrote a tool call as JSON text (`{"name": "explain_access", "parameters": …}`) instead of words. The CSR would see that JSON under **Result**.
- **Guessed account ids:** the Acme and Carol turns often started by calling a tool with an invented account id, which was refused, then looked the account up and tried again. That wastes steps and makes the bad last step more likely.
- **Details were always correct:** they are built only from the tool results, so the facts on screen were right in every run.

The spec makes this evaluation report-only, so it doesn't block P8. But a CSR seeing raw JSON looks broken, so I recommend a small fix before closing.

**Decision: how to handle the wording failures**
- **A. Leave it.** Record it as a known limitation in `production-readiness.md`.
- **B. Small fix (recommended), about an hour, then re-run the evaluation:**
  1. Add to the instructions: "When the CSR names an account, call `find_customer_account` first; never invent an account id."
  2. When the reply text is a tool call written as JSON, show "No summary was written; see Details." under Result instead. This is display-only; the Details stay the source of truth. It gets a unit test.
- **C. Try another Workers AI model** with stronger function calling. This is bigger: it means a new spike and a spec change, since the spec names Llama 3.3 70B.

**Should we explore C? Yes, as a short spike (K5) after B.**
- **Why:** reply wording is the only weakness the evaluation found, and it's a property of the model. Llama 3.3 70B picks tools well but doesn't reliably write a final answer once tools are switched off.
- **Why it's cheap:** the model is one constant in `worker.ts`, and the tools are enforced on the server whatever the model does. Switching doesn't touch security or authorization.
- **How:** run the same evaluation (4 messages × 5 runs, both tool choice and wording) against two or three Workers AI models that support function calling. Candidates I'd check: Llama 4 Scout, Mistral Small 3.1, Qwen3, and gpt-oss-120b. Some of these names may be out of date, so I'd first confirm against the current Workers AI catalog that they exist and support tool calling. I'd time-box it to about two hours.
- **Why after B:** the fixed instructions and the JSON fallback apply to any model. Doing B first means every candidate is compared against the improved Llama, not the current one.
- **What happens next:** if a candidate clearly wins, you decide whether to switch. That means amending the spec (it names Llama 3.3 70B) and recording an ADR. If none wins, we keep Llama and record the comparison as a discovery.

**Renaming "Result":** I recommend **Answer**.
- **Answer:** the model's words answering the CSR's question, which pairs naturally with Details. The fallback would read "No answer was written; see Details."
- **Summary:** honest when it works, but it suggests a summary of Details, and on the Ambiguous and refusal turns it isn't one.
- **Reply:** too generic, since the whole block is the reply.

So B would be:
1. Add the "look the account up first, never invent an id" instruction.
2. Rename Result to Answer.
3. When the text is a tool call written as JSON, show "No answer was written; see Details." This gets unit tests.
4. Redeploy and re-run the evaluation.

Then the K5 spike. Is that plan good to build?

- **B. Switch to gpt-oss-120b (recommended).**
  - It always looks the account up instead of guessing an id. That means no refused calls on invented ids and fewer wasted steps.
  - It always answers in words, and it is faster.
  - Its context window is 128k tokens against Llama's 24k, which matters as a case conversation grows.
  - It costs less per output token ($0.75 against $2.25 per million).
  - Work involved:
    1. Add the plain-text, no-ids and exact-email instruction lines.
    2. Swap the model id.
    3. Amend the spec and `AI_AS_DEVELOPER.md` §1, which both name Llama 3.3, and record the choice in an ADR.
    4. Redeploy and re-run the live evaluation.

    About an hour.

**Decision 2: should the §5 messages say "Acme Corp"?**

"Acme" alone is ambiguous in the seed data, so the expected answer depends on how a model handles ambiguity rather than on choosing the right tool. I recommend naming **Acme Corp** in those two messages. I'd also add one case for bare "Acme" in which either asking which account or checking both counts as a pass.

**1. Free tier: no real difference between the two models.**
- **The allowance:** Workers AI gives 10,000 "neurons" (its usage unit) per day for free, on both the Free and Paid plans. It resets at 00:00 UTC.
- **What happens when it runs out:** on Free, requests fail instead of being billed, so the demo can never cost money. The CSR would see our generic "The assistant could not answer (trace …)" until the reset.
- **Both models run on Free.** I ran gpt-oss-120b on this account today.

| Neurons per million tokens | Input | Output |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Llama 3.3 70B | 26,668 | 204,805 |
| gpt-oss-120b | 31,818 | 68,182 |

A turn is mostly input: the instructions, tool definitions and tool results, resent on each step. My rough estimate is about 5,000 input tokens and a few hundred output tokens per turn. That is roughly 175–200 neurons per turn for either model, or about 50–55 turns a day before the allowance runs out. gpt-oss reads input slightly more expensively but writes output three times more cheaply. Its reasoning counts as output, which roughly evens things out. Enough for a demo, but worth knowing; I'd add a line to `production-readiness.md`.

**2. Lookups by the full name stored in the database**

Today `find_customer_account` matches any fragment of the name (`display_name ILIKE '%acme%'`), so "Acme" finds both Acme Corp and Acme Labs. Requiring the full name means changing that, in four places:

1. **Lookup:** a name matches only the full stored display name, ignoring letter case and surrounding spaces (`lower(trim(display_name)) = lower(trim($1))`), backed by an index. An account id still works as before. Two accounts with the same name would still come back as "several accounts match".
2. **Instructions:** pass the name exactly as the CSR wrote it. If nothing matches, ask the CSR for the account's full name or its id; never guess or complete a name.
3. **Spec:**
   - A new ruling, D14.
   - S07a and S07b change: the "ambiguous name" case becomes two accounts with the same full name.
   - The §5 messages say "Acme Corp", and a bare "Acme" case passes when the assistant asks for the full name.
   - These spec changes need your approval, then RED tests, then the build.
4. **Answers:** already covered. The instructions name accounts by their display name, and Details always shows the name exactly as stored.

**It also improves security.** Fragment search lets an insider list accounts by typing "a", "b" and so on; that's the "Insider enumeration" item in `production-readiness.md`. With exact names, someone must already know an account's full name to find it. That's in line with security over speed.

**One choice for you:** should letter case count? I recommend not: "acme corp" should find Acme Corp, while spelling, spacing between words and punctuation must still match exactly.

My rough estimate for this change is one to two hours, including the spec, tests, deploy and the live re-run. It could be done together with the gpt-oss switch, so there's one redeploy and one evaluation.

Understood: lookups stay permissive, matching any fragment of the name as today, and display must be strict. Lookup doesn't change at all.

**Where display is already strict:** Details, the account cards and the "several accounts match" list all take names from the tool results. Those names come from the database, so Details always shows them exactly as stored.

**Where it isn't:** the Answer is the model's own text. Instructions alone can't guarantee the model won't write "Acme" for "Acme Corp", or `Carol @acme.com` for `carol@acme.com`.

**How to make the Answer strict:**
- **A. Instructions only.** Tell the model: "name every account by its `display_name` exactly as the tools return it; write emails exactly". This is cheap, but a model can always slip.
- **B. A check after the turn.** If the Answer mentions part of an account's name but not the full name, show a warning or the fallback. Detecting "a shortened name" in free text is guesswork, so it would be fragile and give false positives.
- **C. References that the screen fills in (recommended).**
  - The model never writes a name. It writes a reference such as `[acc_AaD0wsQAcISy9edCLE0dkw]`, and for a person `[carol@acme.com]`.
  - The UI replaces each reference with the name stored in the database, taken from that reply's tool results, so names in the Answer are strict by construction.
  - A reference to anything this reply's tools didn't return can't be resolved. It's shown as "an account not in this reply's results" rather than as a name.
  - It also fixes gpt-oss repeating ids: an id becomes the account's name.
  - If the model ignores the format and writes a name itself, that text is shown as written. The instructions make the format the only way to name an account, and the evaluation would count how often the model writes a name instead of a reference (the target is zero).

How C would be built:
1. One instruction line.
2. A substitution step in `replyViewOf`, with unit tests: references resolve to the stored names, unknown references don't resolve, and plain text passes through unchanged.
3. A "named by reference" count in the live evaluation.

It's done together with the gpt-oss switch, so there's still one redeploy. My estimate is under an hour on top of the switch.

One choice for you: should C cover person emails as well as accounts? I recommend yes, since it removes mangled emails like `Carol @acme.com`.

  ### Checked and found sound
  - **Guard order and hardening:**
    - The constructor wrapper runs before the AIChatAgent and Agent wrappers, which I confirmed in the SDK sources.
    - The size check comes first. A chat request is rebuilt from `this.messages` plus the newest user text only. `trigger`, `clientTools` and the custom body are discarded.
    - Clear, tool-result, tool-approval, messages, state and `rpc` frames are dropped, and `validateStateChange` rejects writes that don't come from the server.
    - `_deleteStaleRows` cannot delete stored rows, because the server-generated user id is new each time. This rules out a concurrency race I suspected.
  - **Session on the upgrade (D9, D1, D6):**
    - The Origin check runs before the session is read.
    - The session is verified and the principal reloaded. Assignment is checked, and an unknown case and another CSR's case get the same 404.
    - The forwarded request is rebuilt with only the handshake headers plus the server's `x-connection-session`, so a client cannot inject that header. No `routeAgentRequest` route exists. `get-messages` and other HTTP paths of the DO are unreachable.
    - The cookie is `__Host-`, `SameSite=Strict`.
  - **Tools act as the session principal:**

    - Arguments are strict, so `acting_as` is rejected (S06b). Unexpected failures reach the model and browser only as a generic message with a trace id.
  - **AU-6, D3, D12:** `ReadEnforcement`: the permission gate comes first with one audit record and no data read. A missing subject gets NotFound with no record, matching S08e-C. `disclose` returns Denied only for policy-only reasons. Lookups apply per-candidate decisions and filtering, the no-match `Failed` record, and an HMAC instead of the query.
  - **Authorization:**
    - The decision chain fails closed: an inactive principal gets `permission-not-held` and an unknown action key is denied.
    - A crafted `feature_key` or `operation` containing dots cannot form a granted permission key.
    - D5: the person's decision is not audited.
  - **SQL:** all queries are parameterized, and the ILIKE wildcards `\ % _` are escaped.
  - **XSS:** there is no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`. Text, tool JSON and errorText are rendered as React text.
  - **Rate limiter:** one Durable Object per employee with a sliding window, checked before the expiry and principal checks (S05m).
  - **Verification tests:** S05a–S05h, S05j–S05m, S06a/b, S07a–d, S08a–e and S11/S12 assert exact bodies, close codes, audit records and security events. I found none passing vacuously apart from R1-2.

  - The assignment is checked only at upgrade. Once `case.reassign` exists (case-lifecycle), an open connection held by the previous assignee would keep working until its token expires.

- No verification test passes vacuously, apart from R1-2.

**R1-1 (major, security): a single lookup can list every account.**
- **Cause:** `find_customer_account` checks the query's length before trimming it, and the search has no limit. A query of `" "` becomes `%%` and matches every account.
- **Effect:** one tool call runs a full authorization decision and an audit write per account. It returns every readable account, which is the same gap I'd noted in production readiness, only worse.
- **My recommendation:**
  - Refuse a query shorter than 3 characters after trimming, at the tool, before any use case runs.
  - Search and decide at most 50 accounts per lookup.
  - Return at most 10 candidates. When more than 10 authorized accounts match, add a "more matches, refine the query" flag.
  - **No leak:** the flag counts only accounts the CSR may read, so it says nothing about accounts they can't.
  - **Trade-off:** if more than 50 accounts contain the fragment, some readable ones may be left out without saying so, and the CSR refines the query. With a 3-character minimum, that only matters at production scale.
- **Process:** this changes what the spec promises, so it needs a new ruling, D14, and a new scenario, S07e. That means your approval of the spec change, then a failing test, then the build.

**R1-2 (major, coverage): S05i doesn't prove a connection is closed when its token expires.**
- **Cause:** the test advances the clock and then calls the close routine directly. Nothing checks that connecting actually schedules that close. Deleting the line that schedules it leaves all 367 tests green.
- **Fix:** S05i also asserts that, once the connection opens, a close is scheduled at the token's expiry time (15:20). Changing a verification test needs your agreement, and this one only gets stricter.

**R1-3 (minor): some person references show the model's spelling.**
- **Cause:** `explain_access` echoes the email the model typed, so `CAROL@Acme.com` shows as typed. Owner and admin emails from a lookup are never resolved, so they show as "(a person not in this reply's results)".
- **Fix:** the tool returns the stored, normalized email, and lookup results feed the people the screen can name. I'd add unit tests for both cases.

**R1-4 (minor, logs): refused tool arguments are logged with the full input, including customer emails.** They are also mislabelled as turn failures.
- **Fix:** log a refused argument as `tool-arguments-refused`, with the tool's name and the trace id only. Real failures keep their current log.

**R1-5 (minor): the resume and cancel frames skip D10.**
- **Cause:** they reach the SDK without the expiry check or the principal reload, while D10 says every message reloads the principal. The impact is low: a suspended CSR could only cancel or resume their own reply stream.
- **Fix:** run the expiry and principal checks on those frames too. Frames the guard drops stay silent; they do nothing anyway. I'd record the ruling as a discovery.

**R1-6 (minor, architecture): the 20-messages-a-minute rule lives in an adapter.** Business rules belong inward.
- **Fix:** move it to `kernel/`, where both rate-limiter adapters use it.

**Out of scope:** the reviewer noted two gaps for later:
- once case reassignment exists, an open connection would survive a reassignment;
- signing out doesn't close open connections.

I'd add both to `production-readiness.md`.

**Decisions for you:**
1. Approve the R1-1 spec amendment (D14 and S07e): at least 3 characters, at most 50 accounts searched and decided, at most 10 shown, with the refine flag.
2. Agree to make the S05i verification test stricter (R1-2).
3. Accept the fixes for R1-3 to R1-6 and the two production-readiness rows.

None of this needs the AI. My estimate is about two hours, then the GREEN gate and review round 2.

### Assistant

Nobody outside gets this information. Only a signed-in CSR who already holds `account.read`, in a case assigned to them, could see the B signal. Here is what stands in front of it:

- **Not signed in:** the WebSocket upgrade needs a valid session cookie and the application's Origin, or it's refused before any session is read. Without a connection there are no tools.
- **Signed in, but not on the case:** someone else's case, or one that doesn't exist, gets the same 404.
- **Signed in, but without `account.read`:** the lookup answers "not found" before any search runs (D12), so there's no flag either way.
- **Tools act only as the session's CSR:** the model can't claim to be someone else (S06b).

**Who would learn something under B:** a CSR who may read most accounts but is refused some by policy. In the demo that's a level-1 representative, who can't read Enterprise accounts.

**What they'd learn:** only that at least 50 accounts contain the fragment they typed, some of which they can't read. They wouldn't learn names, ids or how many. The flag only appears past 50 matches, and a CSR can't add accounts, so they couldn't use it to test whether one specific hidden account exists. Every search also writes an audit record for each account it decides, and messages are limited to 20 a minute, so probing leaves a trail.

**Why I still recommend A:** the spec states this as a rule. S07d, "no match and no authorized match look the same", applies AU-6 even to signed-in CSRs: their answers depend only on accounts they may read. B is a small leak, but it would be the first answer that depends on accounts the CSR can't read. Breaking a stated rule for a case the demo data can't trigger seems the wrong trade under security over speed. B is defensible if you'd rather never give a misleading "found". It would need a spec amendment (D14 and S07d), and one more S07e case.

**R2-3** is approved. I'll build it, with one unit test, once you close the discussion. The commit question from my last message is still open: may I commit the review work so far?

Yes, as long as the notice is the same every time. Whether something leaks depends only on whether the message changes with the hidden data.

- **"There may be more" shown only when the cut was hit:** this leaks. The CSR learns that something exists beyond what they see, which is B.
- **The same notice on every name search,** whatever it found: this leaks nothing. It reads the same whether there are 0, 5 or 500 hidden accounts, so it carries no information.

So the proposal is option C:
