4. Errors and fixes
   - **Workers AI allocation exhausted (4006):** 25 eval turns failed at 00:05 UTC. The dashboard shows 0 of 10k used today but enforcement still refuses (yesterday's usage was 14.68k, likely a rolling window). A background probe (`/tmp/ai-probe.mjs`, run from `typescript/system`) checks every 20 minutes. Live evaluations are pending.
   - **`git merge -F -`** isn't supported; used message files instead.
   - **`git add -A`** committed `.probe.tmp.mjs`; removed it with `git rm --cached` and amended. Biome then failed on the untracked probe on main, so it was moved to `/tmp`.
   - **Wrangler deploy "fetch failed"** (IPv6 connect timeout): fixed with `NODE_OPTIONS=--dns-result-order=ipv4first`.
   - **Biome reformatting** repeatedly broke Python search-and-replace anchors: re-read the files and redid the edits; one test file was rewritten whole.
   - **Review rounds** on case-lifecycle events:
     - R4-1: forged event lines through display names; fixed with `quotedName`.
     - R5-1: names went into the audit chain; moved to `case_account_name`; checked Neon (0 records affected).
     - R6-1: a cap on name length; names are cut to 500 characters.
   - **Spike test outside `tests/`** wasn't found by vitest; moved under a `tests` directory. Console output was suppressed, so results were written to a file.

5. Problem Solving
   - **Explain-access and case-lifecycle:** complete and merged. Main is GREEN with 537 tests, deployed and smoke-tested.
   - **Grants-and-approvals spikes:**
     - K1: Workflows wait for an event or time out, can be terminated, and refuse a duplicate create.
     - K2: the scheduled handler can be triggered via `/cdn-cgi/handler/scheduled`.
   - **P2 design decided (not yet coded):**
     - `DecisionRequest` gains an optional `context` (featureKey, quota, grant window start/end, contactAuthenticated, requester/confirmer, approvalGranted) and `contactAccountId`.
     - The PIP computes `duration_days` as the union of days granted for the feature within the last 90 days plus the requested window.
     - The contact access step checks the contact's customer permission (`grant.create` and `grant.revoke` map to `grant.request`) and the contact's holds; otherwise `access.contact-permission-not-held`.
     - The entitlement step judges grant actions: `entitlement.feature-already-included`, `quota-on-unmetered-feature`, `no-subscription`.
     - `grant.revoke` is governed by `grant.create`; `contact-is-authenticated` is likely extended to `grant.revoke` (to be logged as a discovery).
     - The expire wake trusts the timer: it expires an approval if it is Pending.
     - D17 disclosure for changes.

8. Current Work

   Started P2 (decisions with context) of grants-and-approvals on branch `feature/grants-and-approvals`, after P1's RED (41 failing tests). P1's work (migrations, seed, test support, tests, progress/discoveries) is uncommitted. I had just read `decision-attributes.ts` and `PolicyStep`, and was checking what the entitlement computation exposes about grants. The last command, in `subsystem/entitlement/src`, found that `AccountEntitlements` is defined at `compute-effective-entitlements.ts:24`, `SubscriptionRepository` at `subscription-repository.ts:4`, and `model/effective-entitlements.ts` exists. The aim is to compute D5's cumulative grant days and EN-4's checks in the PIP and the entitlement step.

9. Optional Next Step

   Continue P2 as approved ("please continue autonomously as far as you can get"; the plan was approved and the owner said "go for the arch plan" and then accepted the outbox and B′):
   - read `AccountEntitlements` and the grant fields in `subsystem/entitlement/src/usecase/compute-effective-entitlements.ts` and `model/effective-entitlements.ts`;
   - extend `DecisionRequest` with `context` and `contactAccountId`;
   - implement the contact access step (SU-8), the entitlement step's grant checks (EN-4), and D5's day counting in the PIP and policy context attributes;
   - then P3–P8, running tests until the 41 grants-and-approvals tests pass, with the existing 537 tests still green.

Continue the conversation from where it left off without asking the user any further questions. Resume directly — do not acknowledge the summary, do not recap what was happening, do not preface with "I'll continue" or similar. Pick up the last task as if the break never happened.

  I found one High defect: an approved change can be silently lost. The authorization paths largely hold. Paths are relative to `typescript/system/`. "CONFIRMED" means I proved it by reading the code; I wrote no probe tests and did not run the suite.
  
  ### Findings, most severe first
  
  **1. High: an approved change can be lost for good, with the request stuck in `AwaitingApproval`.** CONFIRMED by reading the code.
  - **Where:**
    - `application/entitlement-assistant/src/approval-process-workflow.ts:63-72, 92-108`
    - `subsystem/support/src/usecase/approval-process-steps.ts:204` (`ExpireApproval`: `status !== "Pending"` → `NothingToDo`)
    - `application.ts` `sweep`, which handles only `overdueApprovals` (Pending ones)
  - **Scenario:**
    1. A supervisor approves at T−1s. `decideApproval` passes, since `expires_at > at`.
    2. The decision reaches the Workflow through after-commit delivery or a sweep redelivery. If that delivery fails, or simply arrives after the instance's `waitForEvent` timeout fires at T, the instance takes the "deadline" branch.
    3. `ExpireApproval` sees `Approved` and does nothing, and the instance completes.
    4. The later `sendEvent` reaches a `complete` instance. `#unlessEnded` swallows the error and the message is marked delivered.
    5. Nothing ever calls `ApplyApprovedChange`. The sweep only looks at Pending approvals.
  - **Other routes to the same state:**
    - Any `run()` failure outside a step, such as the rethrown non-timeout error at line 70, leaves the instance `errored`.
    - So does `step.do("apply")` running out of retries during a database outage.
  - **Result:** the card says "Awaiting a supervisor's approval" forever, and the approval no longer appears in the panel.
  - **Rules broken:** D6 ("none is lost between the database and the Workflow"), D8, SU-6.
  - **Your test already shows this state:** `s23-s26-approvals.test.ts:294-308` ("case C") accepts `AwaitingApproval` as the final state when Approved wins against a deadline wake.
  - **Possible fix:** have the deadline wake apply an `Approved` approval, and have the sweep also pick up approvals that are `Approved` but whose request is still `AwaitingApproval`.
  
  **2. Medium: early expiry on any error containing "timed out".** CONFIRMED by reading the code.
  - **Where:** `approval-process-workflow.ts:69` (`/timed out/i.test(String(error))`) and `approval-process-steps.ts:192-216`, where the expiry step "trusts the timer" and never checks `at >= approval.expiresAt`.
  - **Scenario:** any infrastructure error whose text contains "timed out" expires a Pending approval hours early. That includes a storage or RPC "operation timed out" thrown from `waitForEvent`.
  - **Why it matters:** this contradicts discovery 13's claim that "a fault never expires an approval early", and breaks D9.
  - **Possible fix:** a one-line deadline guard in `ExpireApproval`, which would also make any spurious "deadline" wake harmless.
  
  **3. Medium: lock-order inversion between closing a case and the approval paths.** CONFIRMED by reading the code; I did not reproduce a live deadlock.
  - **Close path:** `close-case.ts:94-109` → `PostgresChangeRequestRepository.ofCase` locks the requests (`FOR UPDATE`), then `cancelPending` → `endApproval` updates the approval row.
  - **Other paths lock in the opposite order:** `DecideApproval` (`decide-approval.ts:192-194`), `ApplyApprovedChange` and `ExpireApproval` (`lockedApproval`, `approval-process-steps.ts:31-35`) lock the approval first, then the request.
  - **Scenario:** a CSR closes the case while a supervisor decides the same approval. PostgreSQL detects a deadlock (40P01) and aborts one side, which returns a 500.
  - **Why it matters:** this is exactly the race the hunt list asked about. The data stays correct, but one user gets a server error.
  - **Possible fix:** take the approval lock in the same order everywhere, for example request then approval.
  
  **4. Medium: the change-request read makes no fresh decision.** CONFIRMED by reading the code.
  - **Where:** `get-change-request.ts:37-52`.
  - **What happens:** the endpoint checks only that the caller is the assignee. There is no `grant.create` or `account.read` gate and no audit record.
  - **Scenario:** a CSR whose role was removed, or who no longer passes `enterprise-accounts-need-level-2`, still reads the entitlement diff and parameters of their case's requests.
  - **Rules broken:** D17 ("a CSR who may not read the subject gets 404, as for any read") and CM-16 (fresh permissions).
  - **On discovery 15:** I disagree with keeping this. "What preparing showed" was authorized under permissions that may no longer hold.
  
  **5. Medium: the approvals panel can name accounts the approver may not read.** PLAUSIBLE.
  - **Where:** `decide-approval.ts:63-89, 196-203`. `ListPendingApprovals` returns every pending approval's subject display name, account id and change parameters to any holder of `approval.decide`. `DecideApproval` decides only `approval.decide`, never `account.read`.
  - **Why it matters:** `approval.decide` is its own role ("Approval decision"), so it can be held without `account.read`, or without passing the enterprise level-2 policy. That approver sees and acts on accounts they could not open (AU-4, AU-6).
  - **Spec status:** D7 is silent on this. It is a design gap rather than a deviation from the spec.
  
  **6. Low/Medium: outbox poison messages and a fragile sweep.** CONFIRMED by reading the code.
  - **No retry cap or dead-letter:** `undelivered` picks the oldest 100 unsent messages with no limit on attempts. Once 100 messages fail permanently, newer ones are never redelivered.
  - **Example of a permanent failure:** `#unlessEnded` calls `binding.get()` outside its try (`approval-process-workflow.ts:73`). A `decision` or `terminate` message for an instance that was never created or has been purged therefore throws forever.
  - **No isolation between sweep stages:** in `application.ts` the sweep runs grant expiries, then overdue approvals, then the outbox, one after another with no catch between them. One throw in `recordGrantExpiries` or in any single `approvalProcessWoke` skips all outbox redelivery for that run.
  
  **7. Low: D5 limits can be sidestepped in two ways.** PLAUSIBLE.
  - **New subscription resets the day count:** days are counted only over the latest subscription's grants (`compute-effective-entitlements.ts`, `grants: snapshot.latestSubscription?.grants`). A resubscription or plan change that creates a new subscription would restart the 90-day count, against "on that account".
  - **Quota is checked per grant:** two overlapping 14-day seat grants of +100 each reach +200 with no approval (`grant-quota-up-to-100.yaml` checks each grant alone). The spec does not explicitly make quota cumulative, so the owner should confirm the intent.
  
  **8. Low: a system refusal leaves the request pending forever.** CONFIRMED by reading the code.
  - **Where:** `approval-process-steps.ts:136-145`. If the system identity's `change-request.apply` is refused, the code returns `NothingToDo`.
  - **Result:** the request stays `AwaitingApproval` with no case event and no end state. It should end `Refused`, as a refused re-run does.
  
  ### Areas with nothing serious
  - **Authorization bypass:** none found.
    - Tools only prepare. Grant ids are checked against the subject. Confirm, cancel and get are scoped to the case and its current assignee.
    - Self-approval is blocked for both the requester and the confirmer. Decisions after the deadline are refused, in both the use case and the SQL `expires_at > $4`.
    - The D8 re-run uses the confirmer's current permissions, the contact's authentication as it stood at confirmation, `approval_granted`, the version check and the diff `test` operations, as the spec intends.
  - **Double confirmation, and confirm racing cancel or close:** safe. All three serialize on the case row lock. The D4 replay is correct, and the account version plus the unique `extends_grant_id` index prevent double application across cases.
  - **Existence leaks:** none beyond findings 4 and 5. Missing, other-case and ungated requests all answer 404.

### Assistant

**What round 1 found and what I changed** (all recorded in `discoveries.md`, entries 20–28):
- **High: an approved change could be lost.** If a supervisor's approval reached the Workflow after its 72-hour timer had already fired, the approval process did nothing and ended. The request then stayed "awaiting approval" forever. Now the deadline wake applies an approval that was already approved, and the 15-minute sweep applies any approved change still waiting.
- **Early expiry:** any error whose text contained "timed out" would have expired an approval early. Expiry now also requires the deadline to have actually passed.
- **Deadlock:** a test that closes a case while a supervisor decides its approval failed with a server error ("deadlock detected"). The cause was wider than the reviewer said: both sides also wait on the single audit log row that every record updates. Every path now takes its locks in the same order (case, request, approval, audit record). I repeated the race test five times; it passed each time.
- **Fresh decision on reads:** reading a change request now decides afresh whether the CSR may read the account. A CSR who lost the permission, or may not read an Enterprise account, gets a 404.
- **Approvers:** `approval.decide` is its own role, so an approver could see and decide approvals on accounts they may not read. The approvals list now shows only accounts they may read, and deciding one they may not read answers 404. The spec didn't cover this, so please confirm.
- **Smaller fixes:**
  - each stage of the sweep runs independently, so one failure no longer stops the others;
  - a message the approval process has refused 50 times is left for an operator, with its last error, instead of being retried forever;
  - the 90-day count now spans all of the account's subscriptions;
  - if the system account may no longer apply changes, the request ends "Refused" instead of waiting forever.

**Still open: should the quota limit be cumulative?** The 100 limit is checked per grant, so two overlapping +100 seat grants of 14 days reach +200 with no approval. The day limits count cumulatively; the spec doesn't say whether quota should too. My recommendation: make it cumulative, counting the quota of grants that overlap in time, for the same reason we made days cumulative.

```bash
import { readFileSync } from "node:fs";

await client.query("DROP SCHEMA public CASCADE");
await client.query("CREATE SCHEMA public");
console.log(`dropped ${before.rows[0].tables} tables`);
await client.end();
EOF

U=$(sed -n 's/^NEON_OWNER_URL=//p' .env.neon | sed -E "s/^['\"]//; s/['\"]$//")
DATABASE_URL="$U" NODE_OPTIONS=--dns-result-order=ipv4first npm run -s db:migrate
DATABASE_URL="$U" SEED_PASSWORDS_FILE=.env.seed-passwords SEED_FIXTURE_DAY=2026-10-27 NODE_OPTIONS=--dns-result-order=ipv4first npm run -s db:seed
```

## Prompt — 2026-10-27T03:51:05.073Z

What is this "JudgeGrantChange"? and ChangeApplication? their purpose

### Assistant

Both sit between the decision logic and the grant data, but on opposite sides of the decision.

**`JudgeGrantChange`** (Entitlement subsystem) holds the grant rules the authorization decision needs. It answers two questions:

1. **May this grant exist at all? (EN-4)** Given the account's current entitlements, it returns the reasons a grant is refused:
   - `entitlement.feature-already-included`: the plan already has the feature;
   - `entitlement.quota-on-unmetered-feature`: a quota was given for a feature that has none;
   - `entitlement.quota-required-on-metered-feature`: a metered feature like seats was given no quota;
   - `entitlement.no-subscription`: the account has no subscription in force.
2. **How much does it add up to? (D5)** It returns the days granted for the feature in the last 90 days, counting the requested window, and the quota added by grants that overlap in time. The policies `grants-up-to-14-days`, `grants-up-to-90-days` and `grant-quota-up-to-100` then judge those numbers.

It decides nothing on its own: the decision step for entitlements and the policy step call it. It exists as a use case because Authorization may not reach into Entitlement's model directly (the dependency rule `subsystems-meet-through-use-cases`).

**`ChangeApplication`** (Support subsystem) carries out an allowed change. Its callers decide first; it never decides. It does two things:

1. **When the change is prepared:** it computes the entitlement diff (what the account has now compared with what it would have after the change). That diff is stored on the change request and shown on the card.
2. **When the change is applied,** either at confirmation or by the system after an approval:
   1. it locks the account;
   2. it checks the change is still valid: the account's version hasn't moved, and the diff still matches what the account has now;
   3. if anything changed, it cancels the request as stale;
   4. otherwise it creates, revokes or extends the grant, advances the account's version, and marks the request "Applied".

Confirming and the approval process both share it, so a change is applied the same way whichever path reaches it.

**On the names:**
- **`JudgeGrantChange`:** "judge" overlaps with "decide", which is the decision point's job, and this isn't a decision. I'd call it `GrantRules`, since it is EN-4 plus D5's counting.
- **`ChangeApplication`:** reads as a noun ("an application"), and in this project "application" already means the app layer. I'd call it `ApplyChange`, which reads like our other use cases (`ConfirmChangeRequest`, `DecideApproval`).

Shall I rename them to `GrantRules` and `ApplyChange`?

## Prompt — 2026-10-27T03:53:25.442Z

is it ApplyChangeRequest?

is there some qualification to the rules in GrantRules? what type of rules

everything else is approved; but let's discuss this

### Assistant

**`ApplyChangeRequest`:** yes, that fits our naming, with one adjustment. The class currently does two things:

1. **At prepare time:** it computes the entitlement diff.
2. **At apply time:** it locks the account, checks for staleness, makes the grant change, advances the account's version, and marks the request "Applied".

Only the second is applying. The first is a thin wrapper around Entitlement's `EntitlementDiffs`, so it can move into `PrepareChangeRequest`. What remains is exactly `ApplyChangeRequest`.

One thing to keep clear: `ApplyApprovedChange` already exists, and the two aren't the same kind of thing.
- `ApplyApprovedChange` is what the approval process runs. It makes the system's own decision, re-runs the confirmer's decision, and then calls `ApplyChangeRequest`.
- `ConfirmChangeRequest` also calls `ApplyChangeRequest` when its decision allows the change.

So `ApplyChangeRequest` is the shared last step, never reached without a decision first. Its contract will say so.

**`GrantRules`:** they're Entitlement's rules about grants, and they're of two different types:

| Type | What it answers | Source | Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Eligibility** | Can this grant exist at all, given what the account has? | EN-4 (D11) | Refusal reasons: `feature-already-included`, `quota-on-unmetered-feature`, `quota-required-on-metered-feature`, `no-subscription`. The decision's entitlement step uses them. |
| **Accounting** | How much has been granted, this request included? | D5 | Two numbers: days granted for the feature in the last 90 days, and the quota of grants that overlap in time. The policy step passes them to the policies as `context.duration_days` and `context.quota`. |

The limits themselves (14 days, 90 days, 100 quota) are not in this class: they're policies. Eligibility is a rule that refuses; accounting only produces facts for the policies to judge. Since they're different in kind, I'd split them:

- **`CheckGrantEligibility`:** the EN-4 refusals.
- **`CountGrantedTotals`:** D5's days and quota.

Both are verb-first, like our other use cases. If you'd rather keep one class, `GrantRules` with the contract stating both types would also be fair, but the split makes clear that no limit lives here.

My recommendation is `ApplyChangeRequest` (with the diff moved into prepare), `CheckGrantEligibility` and `CountGrantedTotals`. Which do you want?

### Assistant

The app is ready to demo apart from three things: a dependable model allocation, a way for a reviewer to get in, and the repository's front page (README, prompts).

## What the app does today

Four features are built, deployed from `main` and covered by 607 tests:

| Feature | What a demo shows |
| --- | --- |
| **employee-sign-in** (S01–S04) | Employees sign in with a short-lived, signed session cookie. A suspended or unknown employee is refused with the same answer as a wrong password, so the response doesn't reveal which accounts exist. |
| **explain-access** (S05–S13) | The CSR chats with the assistant: find an account, check an entitlement, list entitlements, and "why can't carol@acme.com create API tokens?". Every answer comes from a tool backed by the decision point, and nothing is guessed. Accounts the CSR may not read return "not found". |
| **case-lifecycle** (S27, S33, S34) | Work happens in a case. The assistant proposes the account it's about and the CSR sets it; the CSR sets and authenticates the customer contact. Events appear as a timeline in the chat. The assistant drafts a closing summary and the CSR closes the case. |
| **grants-and-approvals** (S15–S18, S21–S26, S38, S39) | The assistant prepares temporary feature grants as a card the CSR confirms or cancels. Over 14 days needs a supervisor, who decides in the Approvals section; a Cloudflare Workflow waits up to 72 hours, then applies or expires. Grants can be revoked or extended, and they expire on time. |

Every decision and change goes into a tamper-evident audit chain (each record carries a hash). Policies are written in CEL (Common Expression Language) and versioned. The model can only propose; the decision point decides.

## What isn't built

- **change-subscription (feature 5, planned):**
  - S14: plan changes (upgrade, downgrade, cancel);
  - S16, S16a, S16b: their refusals, staleness, and approvals;
  - S19: prompt injection in customer data as a named scenario (parts are already tested, such as hostile names and injected text in a grant reason);
  - S20: account history from the audit chain.
- **Candidate features, never planned in detail:**
  - S28: courtesy terms;
  - S29: changing a person's role;
  - S30: "What am I allowed to do?";
  - S31: an MCP server for external AI clients;
  - S32: voice input.

For the assignment's four parts (an LLM, a workflow, chat input, memory or state), what's built already covers all four. Feature 5 would add breadth, not something missing.

## How the deployment works

All Cloudflare pieces live in one Worker, described by `wrangler.jsonc`:

| Piece | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| **Worker** | Serves the API and the React UI (static assets) from one origin, and redirects HTTP to HTTPS. |
| **Durable Objects** | `CaseAgentObject` is the assistant of each case (Agents SDK, chat history, WebSocket). `MessageRateLimiterObject` limits messages per employee. |
| **Workflow** | `ApprovalProcessWorkflow` is one instance per approval: it waits up to 72 hours for the decision. |
| **Cron trigger** | Every 15 minutes, records grant expiries and redelivers anything undelivered. |
| **Workers AI** | `gpt-oss-120b`, the model behind the assistant. |
| **Hyperdrive → Neon PostgreSQL** | The system of record: accounts, entitlements, cases, policies, audit chain. |
| **Secrets** | Session signing key, audit hash key, identifier hash key, application origin. They're set with `wrangler secret`, never in the repository. |

A deployment from nothing goes in this order:
1. Create the Neon database and a Hyperdrive configuration.
2. Run the migrations (`npm run db:migrate`).
3. Seed (`npm run db:seed`, with generated passwords).
4. Register the policy set (`npm run policies:register`); the Worker refuses to make decisions until its bundled set is active.
5. Put the secrets.
6. Run `wrangler deploy`.

After that, a code change is just `npm run check`, then `npm run ui:build`, then `wrangler deploy`. A schema change adds a migration. A policy change adds a registration.

## What's needed to show it

| # | Item | Why it matters | Effort |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **Workers Paid plan** | One evaluation plus one live check used up the whole free daily allocation, so a reviewer could hit "could not answer". Only you can enable it. | You, 2 minutes |
| 2 | **Reviewer access** | The passwords are generated and secret. Options: (a) give reviewers one set of credentials in the application, (b) a demo login page with fixed demo accounts, rate-limited and resettable. I recommend (a): nothing new to secure. | Small |
| 3 | **Demo reset** | Demos change data: grants pile up and features become "already included". One command (reset, migrate, reseed, register) returns a clean state before each demo. | Small |
| 4 | **Demo script** | A 5–10 minute walkthrough that hits the strongest points. | Small |
| 5 | **README** | What it is, the architecture, the security decisions, how to run it locally and deploy it, the live link, the demo credentials (if option a), known limits. The repository has no README yet. | Medium |
| 6 | **Prompt history** | The assignment requires it. We planned a redacted export under `tech-docs/`. | Medium |
| 7 | **GitHub repository** | Public. Before pushing: scan the whole history for secrets, and check that the `.env*` files and `.dev.vars` were never committed. | Small |
| 8 | **Quick fixes seen in the live runs** | The model sometimes writes account names next to ids, and once used Markdown bold. It's cosmetic but visible in a demo. A tighter instruction would help, but it costs neurons to evaluate. | Optional |
| 9 | **Video walkthrough** | Fits the documentation work you planned (diagrams, viewpoints). It could start as a recording of the demo script. | Later |

## Can you cap the bill at $5 a month?

As far as I know, no. Workers Paid has no hard spending limit. The $5 is a monthly minimum that includes allowances; usage beyond them is billed. In the dashboard you can set **billing notifications** (an email when usage passes an amount), but those alert, they don't stop anything.

The overage for this app would be small. From memory:
- **Workers AI:** past the 10,000 free neurons a day, it's about $0.011 per 1,000 neurons. Yesterday's heaviest day, 14.68k neurons, would have cost about 5 cents.
- **Workers, Durable Objects, Workflows:** a demo stays well inside the plan's included amounts.

The real risk isn't normal demo use; it's someone abusing the app. We can enforce a cap ourselves:
1. **App-level daily budget (recommended):** count the assistant's model calls per day, and past a limit answer "The demo's daily allowance is used up; try tomorrow." It's a hard cap we control, and it fits what we already have (a rate limiter per employee).
2. **AI Gateway:** put Cloudflare's AI Gateway in front of Workers AI with its rate limit. That cap is per time window, not on dollars.
3. **Accounts:** only people with credentials can chat at all, and messages are already limited per minute per employee.

With 1 and 3, the worst realistic month stays a few cents above $5.

## Worker

**Examples of what isn't static:** the React UI is entirely static: HTML, JavaScript and CSS, built once by `npm run ui:build` and served unchanged. There is no server-side rendering. What the Worker computes per request:
- the API's JSON answers;
- the assistant's WebSocket;
- the HTTP→HTTPS redirect;
- the security headers.

Everything a page shows comes from the browser calling those APIs.

**Languages:** the runtime is **workerd** (V8 isolates; locally, `wrangler dev` runs the same workerd). We use TypeScript, which is the first-class choice. The other options:
- **JavaScript** (same runtime);
- **Python** Workers (supported, younger);
- **Rust**, or other languages compiled to WebAssembly (`workers-rs`);
- **Containers**, for any language in a container, called from a Worker.

Durable Objects, Workflows and the Agents SDK have their richest support in TypeScript, so staying with it is right.

## Durable Objects

There's a dashboard: under Workers you can see each namespace (`CaseAgentObject`, `MessageRateLimiterObject`), its metrics and its instances. I believe the dashboard can now also browse the storage of SQLite-backed objects (ours are SQLite-backed), but please check that in your account.

Otherwise, the data is reached only through the app. In our design that's fine: the Durable Objects hold the chat and rate-limit state, while PostgreSQL holds everything that matters (accounts, cases, grants, audit).

## Account for Cloudflare's reviewers

They need at least **two** logins to show approvals, because nobody may approve their own change. Options:
- **A (recommended):** reuse the fixture employees: Alex (representative, level 1), Blair (representative, level 2) and Morgan (supervisor, level 2). Before you send their credentials, I generate new passwords and reseed. You hand them to Cloudflare in the application, not on the page or in the repository.
- **B:** add dedicated reviewer employees (for example a representative "Riley" and a supervisor "Quinn") to the fixtures. It's clearer in the audit trail, but they're new names for you to approve.

## What I'd do next, in order

1. **You:** decide on Workers Paid and set a billing notification.
2. **Me:**

   - a one-command demo reset (reset, migrate, reseed, register);
   - the reviewer credentials (your choice of A or B).
3. **Me:** README, the prompt-history export, `PROMPTS.md`, the secret scan, and the GitHub repository. The bitscrafter page can link to the repository.

Agreed: the controls come before the paid plan. Here's what to guard against, then the plan. Costs, allowances and product availability below are from memory, so please check them on Cloudflare's pricing pages.

## What can cost money on Workers Paid, and how to cap each

| Cost driver | Who can trigger it | Guard |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Workers AI neurons** (the only real risk) | Anyone signed in, chatting | **G1:** a global daily cap on model calls, set to stay inside the 10,000 free neurons a day, so the extra is $0 by construction. **G2:** a daily message cap per employee. **G3:** bound what each turn can cost. |
| **Worker requests and CPU** | Anyone, signed in or not, even just loading the page | **G5:** limit sign-in attempts per IP address. Signing in deliberately costs CPU (password hashing), so it's the expensive unauthenticated route. Allowances: from memory, 10 million requests a month are included, then about $0.30 a million. |
| **Durable Objects, Workflows, Neon** | Signed-in users only | Bounded by G1, G2 and the existing per-minute limit. A demo stays far inside the included amounts. |

**Your questions:**
1. **G1 is global**, across all users: one counter for the whole app, per UTC day. Past the limit, the assistant answers a fixed "The demo's daily allowance is used up; try again tomorrow" without calling the model.
2. **AI Gateway** shouldn't cost anything extra (its basic features are free), but its rate limit caps requests per time window, not neurons. With G1 it adds little, so I'd skip it.
3. **Yes, cap messages per person too (G2).** It stops one reviewer, or one leaked credential, from spending the whole day's allowance for everyone. A per-minute limit already exists; G2 adds a per-day one.

Other guards worth having:
- **G3, a bounded cost per turn:**
  - a maximum answer length per model call;
  - the step limit we already have (the model can't loop through tools forever);
  - only the latest messages of the conversation sent to the model, since a long chat otherwise costs more every turn.
- **G4, a kill switch:** setting the daily cap to 0 turns the assistant off with one config change. Sign-in and cases keep working.
- **G6, a billing notification (yours, in the dashboard):** an email if usage ever passes, say, $5.50. It's an alarm, not a stop, but it tells you if a guard failed.

**On the static UI:** I only meant it to answer "what isn't static". It matters for cost in one way. With our setting `run_worker_first`, every page and asset load runs the Worker first, so that it can redirect HTTP to HTTPS. Each load counts as a Worker request, which is cheap but not free. G5 covers that route's expensive part.

## Reviewer accounts

These are dedicated employees, same roles as the fixtures, with a `cf-` prefix and strong generated passwords (24 random characters, not the easy-to-type demo pattern):

| Login | Role | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `cf-alex@provider.example` | Representative, level 1 | The basic CSR; can't read Enterprise accounts |
| `cf-blair@provider.example` | Representative, level 2 | Reads Enterprise accounts |
| `cf-morgan@provider.example` | Supervisor, level 2 | Approves changes |
| `cf-sam@provider.example` | Supervisor, level 1 | Shows that a supervisor can't approve their own change |

Their display names would be "Alex (cf)" and so on, so the audit trail tells them apart. The passwords go only into `.env.seed-passwords`; you hand them to Cloudflare privately.

## The plan: a small feature, "usage-guards", same workflow as before

| | Guard | Recommended value | Where |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| G1 | Global model calls per UTC day | **100**: about 25–35 conversations, under 10,000 neurons on our numbers (roughly 140 turns used up the allocation) | A single Durable Object holding the counter, atomic, checked before every model call |
| G2 | Messages per employee per UTC day | **40** | Extends the existing message rate limiter |
| G3 | Maximum answer length per call; history window | **1,024 output tokens**; the **last 20 messages** | The case agent |
| G4 | Kill switch | G1 set to `0` | `wrangler.jsonc` settings, like the approval window |
| G5 | Sign-in attempts per IP address | **10 a minute** | The sign-in route, with a counter per IP address |
| — | Reviewer accounts | the four above | Seed, then reseed |
| G6 | Billing notification | $5.50 | You, in the dashboard |

The values live in `wrangler.jsonc`, so you can change them later without touching code. Tests come first, as usual: each cap stops at its limit, resets the next UTC day, and the kill switch works. Refusals are logged as security events. After deploying, I'll check against the live app that a refused turn costs no neurons.

### Assistant

Before building, one correction that changes what the single variable should be. The 10 million requests are **Worker requests**, a different meter from **Workers AI neurons**, and neurons are the one that can cost you:

| Meter | Included on Workers Paid | What this app uses | Can a demo exceed it? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Worker requests | 10 million a month | A page load is about 5; a chat message about 2; the approvals panel polls every 30 seconds | Practically no: 5 million a month is about 160,000 a day |
| Workers AI neurons | 10,000 **a day**, then about $0.011 per 1,000 | Every model call: a few dozen to a hundred-plus neurons | **Yes**: one 25-turn evaluation used up a day's allowance |

So caps derived from 5 million requests would allow thousands of chat messages a day, enough to run up an AI bill well before reaching the request limit. To guarantee $0 extra, the single variable should be the **daily neuron allowance**. Everything else derives from it:

```text
AI_NEURONS_PER_DAY        = 10000   (the free allowance → $0 extra; raise it if you choose to pay)
NEURONS_PER_MODEL_CALL    = 100     (a conservative estimate; I'll calibrate it from the dashboard after deploy)
model calls per day       = AI_NEURONS_PER_DAY ÷ NEURONS_PER_MODEL_CALL                  → 100   (G1, global)
messages per employee/day = model calls per day ÷ 3 calls per message ÷ PER_PERSON_SHARE → 33 / 4 → 8?
```

That last line comes out too small for a usable demo, so I'll derive it differently. Each employee gets a fixed share of the global cap: `messages per employee per day = model calls per day × 40%`, which gives **40**. One person can use at most 40% of the day, never all of it.

**The request budget:** you can't cap Worker requests economically from inside the Worker. Counting every request centrally would add a Durable Object call per request, and a flood of requests is billed even when refused. The real protections are:
- every costly route needs a signed-in employee;
- the sign-in limit per IP address (G5);
- your billing notification.

If the app later moves to a custom domain, a Cloudflare rate-limiting rule can stop floods before they reach the Worker. I'll still add one setting, `REQUESTS_PER_MONTH_SHARE = 5000000`. It's not enforced, but the README states the app's expected request use, so you can re-plan when you add other projects.

- **Planned change:** change `wrangler.jsonc` and redeploy. It's committed, so it's never undone by accident.

## 1. The architecture test: the problem and the fix

**The rule.** Your constitution's `llm-never-authorizes`: "Every tool call reaches a use case that asks the PDP (the policy decision point) before reading or changing data." This is the heart of "the model proposes; the policy engine decides". The model can only act through tools, so if every tool asks the decision point first, the model can never get around authorization.

**What guards it today.** The per-feature tests check specific situations, for example "a level-1 CSR asking about an Enterprise account gets not found". They prove each existing tool behaves in the cases someone thought to write. They don't prove the rule itself, so nothing fails if, say, a future tool reads accounts straight from the repository without asking. The rule register says an architecture test guards this, but no such test exists. That's the gap: a claim in the rules that nothing enforces.

**Why the usual tool can't do it.** Our architecture rules run through dependency-cruiser, which sees imports, not calls. A tool can import the right use case and still call something else.

**The fix: one test that runs every tool against a decision point that refuses everything.** For each tool the assistant has (`CASE_TOOLS`, ten today), the test:
1. Calls the tool with valid arguments, in the real application against the test database, with the decision point replaced by one that **denies every request and records each request it receives**.
2. Asserts **the decision point was asked** at least once.
3. Asserts **the tool returned a refusal and no customer data**: no account names, emails or entitlements in its output, only "not found", "denied" or a refusal message.
4. Fails for any **new tool not in the test's list**. Adding a tool then forces someone to add it here, with valid arguments. Nothing can skip the check silently.

One exception is listed explicitly with its reason: `draft_case_summary`. It reads no data and changes nothing; it only echoes the text the model wrote, now refusing email addresses.

**What it proves, and doesn't:**

| Proves | Doesn't prove |
| --- | --- |
| No tool reaches customer data without the decision point refusing it first. A tool that skips the question leaks data under "deny everything", and the test fails. | That each decision asks the *right* question with the right action and context. The feature tests (and their mutation checks) cover that. |

**Cost:** about a day's small work, no model calls, so no Workers AI spending; it runs in the normal test suite.

**Possible extension:** apply the same test to the HTTP routes that change data (confirmation, cancellation, the approval decision, the case actions). I'd keep it to tools first, because tools are what the model can call, and add routes if you want the stronger claim.

**The register update:**
- `llm-never-authorizes` would name this test as its guard.
- `state-change-needs-confirmation` would point to grants-and-approvals S18a, S21a and S17a.

Those are the only two register edits.

Shall I build it, tools only or tools and routes?

Fix: "at most 100 messages, and 100 approvals of each kind, a run".
  - **architecture.mdx:304-305**, "Hyperdrive, its cache off": true, but none of the cited sources say it. The source is `typescript/system/README.md:45-51` (`--caching-disabled`, so the principal is loaded fresh). This matters to a security reader, because the per-request reload of the principal depends on it.
  - **architecture.mdx:334**, binding `MESSAGE_RATE_LIMITER` "used by the message guard, the sign-in": incomplete. It also rate-limits account search (worker.ts:125-128, prefix `account-search:`).
  - **security.mdx:212-214** is overstated:
    - The model never writes a record. `draft_case_summary` "stores nothing" (tools.ts:572); the representative submits the summary at closure.
    - The closure route checks only length, 1 to 4,000 characters (application.ts:255; 002-case-lifecycle.sql:21-23). Nothing checks for an email address there.
    - So "it carries no one's details" is the intent, not something enforced.
    - Fix: "The model only drafts a case's summary, and its draft tool refuses a draft holding an email address; the representative reviews and submits it."
  - **security.mdx:258**, outcome "Allow, Conditional or Deny": a record's outcome can also be `Succeeded` or `Failed` (001-audit-chain.sql:10).
  - **security.mdx:253-262**, "the record's fields, as the hash covers them": the hash also covers `audit_chain_record_id` and `detail` (audit-record-hash.ts:41, 53), and the table leaves both out.
  - **security.mdx:262**, "the key that signed it": an HMAC is a keyed MAC, not a signature. The project's own ADR insists on this: tamper evidence, not non-repudiation (adr/attribution.md:79). Use "the key of its HMAC".
  - **security.mdx:224**, "breaks every hash after it": add "for anyone without the key". Line 242 says this later, but this sentence reads as absolute.
  - **security.mdx:233**, "ids, codes and roles alone": nearly right. `detail` also holds change parameters, authentication kind and method, and keyed HMACs of search queries and failed sign-in identifiers (find-customer-account.ts:122, sign-in.ts:86, authenticate-contact.ts:75). Names are kept outside the chain behind a `name_id` (set-case-contact.ts:42-52; production-readiness.md:32). Suggest "ids, codes, roles and keyed hashes, never a name or an email". The "no personal data" claim itself holds.
  - **security.mdx:271**, "each must answer a refusal": `GET /api/cases/{case_id}` answers 200 by design, with the accounts unreadable (test lines 76-78, 87; discoveries.md:44). Suggest "a refusal, or a view with what Drew may not read left out".
  - **security.mdx:188**, "reasons only to who may read the account": the condition is stricter. Reasons go only to a holder of `account.read` who was stopped by policies alone (disclosure.ts:17-22). Lines 79-83 say it correctly; tighten the table row.
  - **security.mdx:190**, "every decision is recorded": lines 292-294 of the same article say an allowed approvals listing is not recorded. Fix: "every decision on customer data is recorded".

  **3. What an IAM security reviewer would object to**
  
  - Calling an HMAC a signature (line 262), and the unconditional "breaks every hash".
  - The summary claim at security.mdx:212-214: the email check exists only on the model's draft, not on what gets stored.
  - "Every decision is recorded" next to the paragraph that admits allowed approvals listings are not.
  - The cache-off claim cites sources that don't mention it. The reload-every-request guarantee depends on it, so a reviewer may check.
  - The reset (section 4) runs from the owner's workstation with the Neon owner URL, and seeding bypasses the audit (production-readiness.md:154, 157). That's fine for a demo. "The system runs itself" is close to this sentence, so the "outside the Worker" wording below helps.

  "Drops and rebuilds the database and its fixtures" is loose:
  - It does not drop the database. It runs `DROP SCHEMA public CASCADE` and `CREATE SCHEMA public` (demo-reset-command.ts:29-30).
  - It then migrates, seeds with the passwords file and today's date as the fixture day, and registers the bundled policy set (lines 34-37).
  - It refuses to run without `--yes`, `DATABASE_URL` and `SEED_PASSWORDS_FILE` (lines 17-24).
  - The runbook calls it optional (demo-runbook.md:57).
  - The file's own doc comment says "drops every table" (line 9). The code drops the whole schema, which is broader.

Cases, grants, approvals and the audit chain are all replaced. The project's runbook says when and how."
  
  **Figure 30 (audit chain)**
  
  It matches the attribution subsystem in substance: `previous_hash` is h(n-1); h(n) is HMAC-SHA-256 under the key named by `hash_key_id` over the RFC 8785 JSON of the record; the head row is locked FOR UPDATE; the record is written in the action's own transaction; no personal data. Two small issues:
  - "append only: the role may not UPDATE or DELETE" should say "may not UPDATE or DELETE a record". The application role does have UPDATE on `audit_chain_head` (001-audit-chain.sql:50-51). No migration grants DELETE anywhere.

  The field list in the boxes is a fair abbreviation of a record.

The first reviewer (architecture and security) reported. Its main findings:

- **"Breaks every hash after it":** the page states it unconditionally; it should add "for anyone without the key".
- **The case summary:** the page says the summary "carries no one's details". In fact only the model's draft is checked for email addresses. A summary the CSR types or edits before closing isn't checked, so that claim describes the intent, not something enforced.
- **"Every decision is recorded":** contradicted a few paragraphs later, where the page itself says an allowed approvals listing isn't recorded.
- **The demo reset:** "drops and rebuilds the database" is loose. It drops the database's public schema, migrates, reseeds dated to today, and registers the policy set.
- **Smaller corrections:** the sweep handles up to 100 approvals of each kind per run (not 100 in total); outcomes also include Succeeded and Failed; the cache-off claim needs a citation; and a few line ranges are off.

I've sent bitscrafter-7b one review of its draft series. Most claims hold, but several need fixing before it ships.

**The most serious, for a Cloudflare reviewer:**
- **The Enterprise example is false.** The page says a level-1 rep asking about Cyberdyne gets "not found". In fact they're told "denied by policy", with the reason. "Not found" only goes to someone with no right to read accounts at all.

- **Absolutes contradicted by the code or the page itself:**
  - "every decision is recorded";
  - "a held account is refused everything";
  - "every tool";
  - "a status moves only forward";
  - a claim of resistance to prompt injection, where the real protection is that every effect needs a person's click;
  - a remedy suggesting a role, which no code computes;
  - a plan upgrade that isn't built.
- **Live check:** "Passed all 13 steps" leaves out that the first run found a gap, which was fixed before the second run passed.
- **Review process:** the page says review agents checked the plan and tests. In fact only the adversarial review ran as a separate agent.

**Our repository had two of those errors as well,** and I've fixed them (`2661b71`, documentation only):
1. Scenario S5 said "not found" for a level-1 rep; it now says "denied by policy".
2. Scenario S4 promised a role as a remedy; it no longer does.
3. In the README, the claims about tools and refusals are now exact: "every tool that reads or changes data", and what each kind of refusal reveals.

**For the page:** cite `2661b71` for documentation. The deployed version is unchanged (`e95fc7c4`), since no code changed.

## 2. Why refuse an email address in the summary at close?

It's about privacy rather than access control: the case summary is stored and readable by everyone who may read that customer's account, now and later. Today that's every support rep, and in production it would include people who join later.
- **The risk:** a contact's email or other contact details written into the summary spread personal data far beyond what the case needed. If a rep pastes "Jane at jane@acme.com asked…", her address is then visible to everyone who opens Acme's history.
- **What the check adds:** data minimization, enforced rather than only advised. The panel already says "keep it about the subject only"; the check would make the email part a rule. It also makes the page's original claim true.
- **What it costs:** a rep who has a legitimate reason to write an email can't. In this app that's rare: the contact is already recorded on the case, with their own record and access rules.
- **The limit:** like the draft check, it catches email addresses, not names.

My recommendation is yes, given the project's IAM focus. It's still a judgment call, so it's your choice.

## 3. The export

Both sessions are rendered: Oct 22–25 and Oct 26 to now, about 22,000 lines in all.

| Kept | Left out |
| --- | --- |
| Your prompts, my replies, one line per tool call | Tool outputs (file contents, command output) |
| Context summaries, labeled | System reminders and background-task notices; images (noted as "an image") |
| Messages from the bitscrafter sessions about this project's page, labeled with the session's name | Messages from sessions of your other projects (a note says one was left out); their names become "[another project]" |
